#153. US tech stocks see sharp falls as AI spending nears $1tn

Tech stocks tumbled for the second day in a row as concerns rise over the hundreds of billions of dollars firms are investing in artificial intelligence (AI).

Shares in Intel plummeted by 28% on Friday, while Google owner Alphabet saw its stock price continue to fall another 1.4% after a 12% plunge on Thursday.

Other tech stocks, including Reddit and Amazon also fell, while the Nasdaq index of the largest tech firms listed in the US, ended October with its worst monthly slide since March 2025.

While tech firms are spending big on AI, their earnings are not always meeting analysts' expectations, spooking investors and creating fears of an AI bubble.

Major tech companies have ramped up artificial intelligence spending, with Meta, Alphabet, Oracle, Amazon, and Microsoft committing to spending $800bn between them in 2026.

Alphabet's stock market value fell sharply on Thursday, taking more than $500bn (Â£374bn) off its market value after it revealed the AI summaries it now provides as the top answer to search queries were affecting its core search advertising revenue.

At the same time, Alphabet expects to spend up to $205bn this year on investments, almost entirely related to AI.

The bigger losses on Friday were elsewhere however, with Intel shedding more than $130bn in value.

The chip maker said it planned to increase spending on AI despite weaker demand for computers, a major source of demand for its micro-processors.

On Thursday, Intel's finance chief David Zinsner said the firm did not expect to bring in more cash than it spent this year and forecast lower-than-expected revenue for its fourth quarter.

"Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year," he said in the chip maker's third quarter earnings report.

In the earnings call, he said the firm had significantly ramped up investment in equipment in the third quarter and expected that to be "even more significant" in the following three months.

Amazon on Thursday revealed it had also spent more cash than it was making, and that spending had increased six-fold over the span of three months.

The company has committed to spending $220bn this year, putting the money towards investments such as AI data centres, network equipment, and servers.

Amazon shares recovered some overnight losses, but were still down by 1.4% at the end of Friday trading.

Reddit stock plunged by 29%, after the social media platform revealed weaker-than-expected daily user figures and warned that Google's growing AI-driven search was hurting referral traffic.

Stock in online game platform Roblox also fell, down by 18%, as its daily active user numbers dropped.

Several major tech firms have reported earnings this week, including Microsoft, and analysts said there were questions about the payout from the huge investments in AI.

Chris Low, from investment management firm FHN Financial, said the bulk of what was happening in the stock market was in reaction to some of the biggest tech players such as Amazon and Google.

He told Reuters the companies were "showing some pretty significant growing pains as they aggressively pursue the AI build-out".

"The thinking is that these signs suggest that the entire sector is going to slow down."

Many analysts have been debating the possibility of an AI bubble in the stock market.

The worry is that some tech firms will not be able to justify their sky-high valuations because the cost of building AI data centres and developing the software will end up being greater than the money they can make from it.

Kathleen Brookes, research director at trading firm XTB, said there have been many ups and downs in AI trade in recent months.

"Investors have turned ruthless, punishing any signs of disappointment in [third quarter] earnings," she said.

"The AI trade has had a good run in recent years, but now investors want payback, and companies like Alphabet and Intel are in the crosshairs."

However, stock market values for many of these companies remain well above where they were a year ago.

While the Nasdaq closed 2.8% lower, it is up 7.8% over the last year. Similarly Amazon is up nearly 8% over the past 12 months, while Alphabet is down 1.3%.

Brookes noted that November is typically a strong month for stocks.

"If we see AI stocks continue to struggle in the coming days, it could suggest the AI trade is past its peak, and investors will be looking for a new theme to drive markets," she said.

#152. I bonds now pay 5.51% — but there are trade-offs. What to know before buying

Now that Series I bonds pay higher interest, the government-backed, nearly risk-free asset could be more attractive for certain investors. But I bonds have downsides, and there are alternatives to consider.

Newly purchased I bonds currently pay 5.51% annual interest through April 30, up from the 4.26% yield offered on purchases made through October.

Amid rising inflation, the variable part of I bond interest rose to 4.08%, while the fixed part increased to 1.40%, the highest since 2006. Combined, the 5.51% composite rate is the biggest yield since November 2022.

The new I bond rate is "very attractive," according to Ken Tumin, founder of DepositQuest.com, a blog that tracks I bond rates, among other deposit accounts.

The variable part of I bond interest resets every six months, based on the consumer price index. For the next six months after purchase, I bonds look "very competitive" compared to other shorter-term options for cash, such as savings accounts or certificates of deposit, according to Tumin.

Meanwhile, the I bond's 1.4% fixed rate stays the same for as long as you own it, which could appeal to longer-term investors, he said.

But I bonds aren't right for everyone. Here are some key things to know before purchasing.

Investors can buy $10,000 of electronic I bonds per person per calendar year via their TreasuryDirect account. You can open additional TreasuryDirect accounts for other family members or businesses to buy more.

I bond interest is tax-deferred until redemption and is not subject to state or local taxes. You can also avoid federal taxes if you use the money for qualified education expenses.

But experts say there are some downsides. The funds are locked up for 12 months from purchase, and you'll lose your last three months of interest if you redeem within five years.

Advisors typically recommend keeping at least three to six months of living expenses in cash for emergency savings. I bonds could boost those reserves, experts say, as long as you keep enough readily accessible cash elsewhere.

"I always say [I bonds are] a secondary emergency fund," said David Enna, founder of Tipswatch.com, a website that tracks Treasury inflation-protected securities, or TIPS, and I bond rates.

Once you own I bonds for five years, you can redeem the money as needed, penalty-free.

With I bond purchase limits, restrictions and some TreasuryDirect complaints, advisors often recommend other options for clients who want to preserve purchasing power.

"For most of my clients, I bonds are more hassle than they're worth," said certified financial planner Eileen Olson, founder of advisory firm Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina. "The amount is too small to matter."

On a $2 million portfolio, the $10,000 annual purchase limit is only 0.5%, and it earns about $550 in a year before federal taxes, she said. "That won't protect anyone's lifestyle from inflation."

Instead, Olson prefers TIPS, which don't have purchase limits and can be bought alongside other assets, outside TreasuryDirect.

TIPS have five-, 10- and 30-year terms, with yields ranging from about 2.3% to above 3% for the 30-year term, as of Nov. 2.

However, the right option depends on your investing goals and timeline, experts say.

"For short-term cash, Treasury bills and high-yield savings are simpler," Olson said.

Also backed by the U.S. government, Treasury bills, or T-bills, have terms ranging from four weeks up to 52 weeks. As of Nov. 2, some T-bill yields were well over 4%.

By comparison, the top 1% average of high-yield savings accounts were paying above 4%, according to rate comparison site DepositAccounts. Some one-year certificates of deposit were also offering rates over 4%, the same day, according to Bankrate.

Money market funds are another option. Some of the biggest funds were paying around 4%, as of Nov. 2, according to Crane Data.

#151. Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Two major blockchain analytics firms say they have robust intelligence pointing to a North Korean state-backed group as the likely culprits in the $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchange.

The Lazarus Group, a North Korean hacking unit also known as TraderTraitor, was identified "with high confidence" by TRM Labs, as well as Chainalysis.

This incident is just the latest linked to the country, one of the poorest in the world and where access to the internet is severely restricted.

Experts accuse the government of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un of relying on cybercrime to finance itself, something Pyongyang has long denied.

Though not financial, the nation's most spectacular suspected hack took place in 2014. The target: Sony Pictures, which had made the Hollywood film The Interview. The film involves a plot to assassinate Kim.

The resulting hack exposed thousands of embarrassing emails exchanged among the studio's top executives.

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

Crypto giant Binance attempting to recover $6.3bn stolen in hack linked to North Korea

Last year, the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) said North Korea was responsible for the theft of $1.5bn on the Bybit exchange.

In the aftermath of that incident, the agency said TraderTraitor had moved to quickly launder and disperse the stolen assets, converting them to Bitcoin and other digital assets with a goal of eventually turning them into hard currency.

The FBI has broadly accused North Korea of "illicit cybercrime and virtual asset theft activities".

The nation has systematically developed its cyber operations, recruiting the most promising students from universities and channeling them into hacker groups overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, according to reports.

Some of the cyberwarfare conducted takes place just over the nation's border in China, according to analysis from tech groups.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Much of its hacking activity has centred on monetary gain.

North Korean hackers are also suspected in the theft of $620m worth of cryptocurrency in 2022.

In this most recent heist, North Korean agents are accused of using a special authorisation to turn a Binance hot wallet, which is connected to the internet, into one that moved funds automatically to the attacker.

TRM said it's likely the attacker gained access to Binance's hot wallet signing system as part of the scheme.

Binance has not directly responded to the BBC's queries about the incident's potential links to the area.

The company had initially said it thought an engineer's laptop had been compromised, allowing unauthorised withdrawals, but later said the attack was wider.

"Our subsequent investigation has uncovered additional points of breach," Binance's co-CEO Richard Teng said.

Some 80% of stolen assets have been secured, a Binance spokesperson said on Friday, but the rest remains unaccounted for.

If the accusations are true, the record-making heist shows how powerful suspected North Korean hackers have become â a sign that the tools of the hacking trade have grown sharper.

Experts say the rise of artificial intelligence has also given threat actors new ways to carry out crime, through automation and other means.

In this case, it's plausible that AI was used to infiltrate Binance's systems.

As Chainalysis put it, the attacker used laundering patterns "we have not seen before".

#150. No more washing up: How Airbnb's new house rules have divided hosts and guests

Airbnb host Lisa says her Welsh holiday let was left 'a complete mess' by a group of guests

When Lisa Clewes returned to her Welsh holiday let after a group of guests checked out, she was horrified by the mess.

Just days earlier, she had met the group in person and helped with their luggage.

But after a dinner party, the house was left "in a complete mess" with broken glasses, drinks cans on the floor, pans full of food on the table and fish guts left in the garden.

"It was really unbelievable. Had I had another booking the same day, I would have had to cancel or delay check-in," says Clewes.

She charges a cleaning fee that covers around 75% of the cost of a cleaner and asks guests to empty the fridge, put rubbish in the bins and load the dishwasher.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her property and fears that guests leaving more mess could mean increasing costs

But Airbnb's new rules mean hosts can no longer ask guests to do jobs normally carried out by cleaners, including washing and putting away dishes, stripping beds and taking rubbish off site.

The updated guidelines, which apply to bookings made globally from 20 November, state that hosts can now set seven standard house rules and add other "reasonable" requests.

Clewes says it typically takes cleaners four hours to turn around her three-bedroom property. She fears guests leaving more mess could mean hiring extra cleaners and increasing costs â expenses that may ultimately be passed on to her guests.

The changes have come as a relief to guests like Jessica Newhook, who says she has been faced with lengthy checkout lists, often on top of hefty cleaning fees.

She booked an Airbnb in Florida for a family reunion of 11 guests and says they had to "leave the property move-in ready for the cleaners", despite paying a $250 (Â£187) cleaning fee.

Newhook says the host told them to wash and fold towels, clean the bedding and kitchen, and sweep and mop the floors. She was also warned by the host that their behaviour needed to improve or they could be told to leave, because they had left chairs blocking bins.

"We had rules about everything and it was not relaxing at all."

While Newhook likes the home comforts of staying in an Airbnb, she says the combination of relentless rules and rising cleaning fees has taken away much of its appeal.

"It is getting to the point where a hotel is cheaper and I know what to expect."

Krissi Foskett, 46, from Glasgow, runs a national cleaning company and regularly stays in Airbnb properties so "can see the issue from both sides".

Krissi says there's a difference between being a respectful guest and being expected to help run the business you've just paid to stay in

During a recent stay in Stafford, she paid a Â£35 cleaning fee but was still asked to strip the beds, put on a wash, empty bins and carry rubbish down a long track to a recycling area.

"I don't particularly mind doing those things, but it did make me question what exactly I was paying the additional cleaning fee for," she says.

For Foskett, there is a difference between being a considerate guest â "putting your dirty dishes in the dishwasher" â and being "expected to prepare the property for the next guests".

Through her cleaning business, she has also seen the other extreme, with teams arriving to find broken furniture, vomit and pet urine.

While she sympathises with hosts facing tight turnarounds and rising cleaning costs, these should not be passed on to guests, she says.

"If you're running an Airbnb as a business, professional cleaning is a cost of doing business."

Anthony Marsh, who co-owns a cabin retreat in North Yorkshire with his husband, used to list five of their 12 properties on Airbnb, but says the new rules have prompted him to reduce this number to just two.

His concern with the changes is that "Airbnb has effectively given guests permission to come and leave properties a tip, and a host can't do anything about it".

Anthony and his husband used to list five of their 12 cabins in North Yorkshire on Airbnb...

... but will now list just two in light of the new rules

He believes asking guests to wash their pots and put out the bins is hardly unreasonable and, with a small cleaning team and tight turnaround times, he worries extra mess could leave properties unready for the next arrivals.

"It's common sense and common decency," he says.

His company does not charge a cleaning fee, and Marsh says most guests are respectful. But fees are more common than not. Some 63.2% of Airbnb listings charge a cleaning fee, with an average cost of Â£55 according to data from AirROI, external.

Laura Lewis runs a directory that helps holiday-let owners market their properties and connect with potential guests.

Many of her clients also list on Airbnb and she says most are unhappy with the new rules, arguing that asking guests to wash up is a matter of basic respect.

"Some people say it's factored into the cleaning fee, but I wouldn't leave a holiday let with all the washing-up to do. It's not the right thing," she says.

Laura Lewis says it's a matter of "basic respect" to wash dishes and take the bins out

The announcement has added to growing frustration among owners following increases in Airbnb's commission rates last year. Many are investing in their own websites and social media to attract direct bookings and avoid platform fees.

Lewis has taken the same approach with the three treehouses on her farm â most of her bookings are already direct and she now plans to remove her listings from Airbnb altogether.

Not all hosts are concerned about the changes, however. Matt Porsz, who hosts and manages other Airbnb properties full-time, believes the update will improve the guest experience without changing how most people behave.

Guests at his property in south-west London are asked only to lock up and return the keys. "People sometimes strip the beds or do things without me asking, and that's nice for my cleaners, but I don't expect it."

Porsz doubts the changes will lead to guests leaving properties in a worse state, pointing to Airbnb's mutual review system.

"If I put a big chore list, I wouldn't expect a five-star review and vice versa â if a guest leaves the place in a state, you're not going to give them a good review."

He believes the system encourages both sides to behave reasonably, and that most guests will continue to treat properties with respect.

"Airbnb is trying to cut out the silly stuff because it's not a good guest experience."

#149. Don't get caught out by new Buy Now Pay Later rules

If you've bought something via Buy Now Pay Later (BNPL) since the summer, then you'll have been tested to see if you can afford it.

BNPL has always been a type of loan, usually paid back in instalments, but it's only been officially regulated since July.

This means stricter background checks on your ability to repay.

If you fail, then your purchase via BNPL will be blocked. If there's a chance of this happening to you, here are some ways to approach the situation.

BNPL lenders - such as Klarna, Clearpay and others - must now have regulator approval to operate, giving shoppers more access to refunds and independent rulings on complaints if things go wrong.

But new affordability checks on each BNPL transaction could also leave you blocked from services for the first time.

There are different affordability checks for different purchases, partially based on how much is borrowed.

But, with the expensive festive season approaching, here's how to avoid being blocked when time and finances are tight.

Here are four things to consider if you find your account is closed or your borrowing limit has been massively reduced, from Sara Williams, a former volunteer debt adviser at Citizens Advice who posts tips as Debt Camel on social media.

Slow down your purchases: is what you are buying essential or can you postpone it?

Be more careful about what you buy to avoid needing a high credit limit to, for example, buy lots of clothes in different sizes

Cancel subscriptions to escape the trap of having to use BNPL to make up for the cost of previous payments

Talk to debt charities for help and access to food bank vouchers if you are using BNPL for essentials

If you are struggling to make repayments on any type of loan - BNPL or otherwise - you should seek help before things spiral out of control.

The government-backed, independent, MoneyHelper website, external has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

It also suggests writing down exactly what you owe, to help plan how you will repay the money.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and a bill prioritiser, external to show which payments to make first and what to do if you miss a payment.

You should also be sceptical of supposed debt solutions you might see on your social feeds.

#148. Rivian third-quarter earnings beat estimates as software revenue, R2 deliveries rise

Rivian Automotive beat Wall Street's third-quarter earnings expectations as it ramped up production of its new R2 entry-level SUV that the company has promised investors will lead it to profitability.

Here's how Rivian performed in the period ended Sept. 30, compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Shares of Rivian were up roughly 7% during afterhours trading Thursday, after hitting a new 52-week low of $11.38 earlier in the day. The stock closed at $11.45 a share, down 1.9%

The electric vehicle maker also revised its 2026 guidance to mark expected improvements in its vehicle deliveries, pre-tax adjusted earnings and capital expenditures.

The new guidance calls for a pre-tax adjusted loss of between $1.75 billion and $1.85 billion — a $100 million improvement at the midpoint from its prior guidance — vehicle deliveries of between 67,000 and 70,000 units, up from at least 65,000; and capital expenditures of between $1.4 billion and $1.5 billion, $300 million less at the midpoint.

Rivian attributed the improvement in its pre-tax adjusted earnings guidance to better-than-expected revenue related to its sales of regulatory credits, an IEEPA tariff refund receivable in the third quarter and its expected increase in vehicle deliveries.

The R2 — the first deliveries of which arrived to customers in June — has become Rivian's best-selling model and was responsible for "much of the gain quarter-over-quarter," the company said.

Rivian delivered a record 19,248 units from July through September. The company does not disclose deliveries by vehicle.

The EV maker remains unprofitable overall but continued to narrow its losses during the quarter. The company reported a net loss attributable to common stockholders of $913 million, compared with a loss of $1.17 billion during the third quarter of 2025.

Rivian's gross profit, which is closely monitored by investors, was $176 million during the third quarter, up from $24 million a year earlier. Its adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was a loss of $435 million, a $167 million improvement from the same period last year.

The company reported that its software and services segment continues to grow, generating $605 million in revenue and a gross profit of $256 million, a 42% margin. That compares to its automotive operations, which recorded nearly $1.55 billion in revenue and a gross loss of $80 million.

Also on Thursday, Rivian reiterated that it expects the R2, a smaller and less expensive sibling to its current luxury R1S SUV, to have a positive gross margin by the end of the year. CEO RJ Scaringe has said the company needs to scale beyond current R2 production plans of 160,000 units in order to achieve overall profitability.

"That ramp-up is critical for us getting to positive gross margin on R2 and that ultimately will contribute to positive gross margin for the business," Scaringe told CNBC's Phil LeBeau during an interview Thursday. "We're going to see that through the end of the quarter and the unit economics will continue to grow … into Q1 of next year as well."

Rivian on Thursday said its cash, cash equivalents and short-term investments balance was $5.5 billion, up from $5.3 billion to end the second quarter.

#147. Virgin Media O2 set to cut thousands of jobs to reduce costs

Thousands of Virgin Media O2 staff are facing redundancy as the telecoms firm attempts to save money in the face of billions of pounds of debt and tougher competition.

The broadband provider has not finalised the number of roles to be cut but it is likely be a sizeable chunk of its 16,000-strong workforce, the BBC understands.

Virgin Media O2 has billions in debt on its balance sheet and is tackling both smaller providers as well as giants like BT, which has agreed a deal to acquire rival TalkTalk, which Virgin Media O2 has firmly opposed.

The company declined to comment. Prospect, a union which represents Virgin Media O2 staff, said the cuts were "disappointing and very unsettling".

Noel McClean. deputy general secretary at Prospect, said the union was "committed to supporting our members through what is a difficult time, as well as also working with employers to explore ways to mitigate the number of redundancies and ensure retraining and redeployment of staff affected".

The majority of the affected staff are in roles that are not customer-facing and a number of senior leadership changes are already under way, the BBC has been told.

Virgin Media O2 is also expected to review its use of contractors and suppliers in conjunction with the job cuts.

As of 30 June, Virgin Media O2's total liabilities stands at Â£27.2bn while it holds assets worth Â£45.9bn, according to the firm's latest accounts.

Meanwhile, the company is dealing with fierce competition from cheaper "altnets" like Hyperoptic, Community Fibre, Gigaclear and YouFibre.

Virgin Media O2 had attempted to respond to this growing threat by buying altnet competitor Netomnia for Â£2bn via nexfibre, a joint venture owned by Virgin Media O2's owners.

However, the Competition Markets Authority (CMA) has provisionally found competition concerns in the deal, external, with a final decision to come in December, sparking anger from Virgin Media O2.

In October, Virgin Media O2 called the BT's agreed takeover of TalkTalk a "stitch-up" that had allowed its rival to "tighten its grip" over the market.

BT said its deal provided "a safety net" for TalkTalk customers as their broadband provider faced collapse without the takeover.

On Friday, BT's share price jumped 7% after the Financial Times reported Virgin Media O2 was close to agreeing a deal, external to use its rival's fibre broadband network.

The BBC has been told the deal was very close and that Virgin Media O2 was highly likely to announce it will stop all investment in fibre in its results on Tuesday.

#146. Brookfield plans for a $27 billion AI data center at UK power station

Brookfield is targeting a massive artificial intelligence data center in the north of England, as Europe scrambles to develop infrastructure to support the rollout of AI amid high energy and land costs.

Energy company Drax Group said Tuesday it has signed an agreement with Brookfield and the asset manager's neocloud platform Radiant to jointly develop an AI data center on the Drax Power Station estate in North Yorkshire. London-listed shares of Drax were up 8.8% in early morning trading.

The project is initially targeting up to 100 megawatts of capacity to be operational from the first quarter of 2028, with an additional 500 MW of capacity to be brought online between 2028 and 2031. It is then looking to secure more than 1 gigawatt of capacity at the site by an unspecified date.

Brookfield estimates total investment, including semiconductors, could exceed £20 billion ($26.8 billion), with an initial £2.5 billion investment in phase 1.

"This is a unique opportunity for the UK's AI ecosystem to build at scale," Radiant CEO Mahdi Yahya said in a statement. "By designing, building and operating from the UK, it will give British researchers, startups and enterprises access to frontier compute at home."

The first phase of Brookfield's project in North Yorkshire will use power from the grid and target commercial customers from early 2028.

The initial phase is subject to planning, regulatory and network approvals from the government and relevant agencies, alongside securing a tenant for the project, Drax said in a statement said. The company added that final investment decisions are yet to be made.

While the U.S. has surged ahead on developing the huge data centers needed to power AI, Europe has been slower to break ground on the facilities. The U.K. in particular has struggled with high energy costs and challenges with long waiting times for businesses to connect to the grid.

In October, it was reported that Microsoft had pulled out of a deal to anchor an Nscale data center just outside London, amid delays connecting to the country's power grid. An Nscale spokesperson told CNBC at the time that it remained "committed" to the project and was "exploring alternative power sources for the site."

Unlike the U.K., the Nordics benefit from abundant power and available land. Countries in the region, like Finland, have seen major commitments to develop AI data centers this year. In September, Google said it would invest at least 13 billion euros ($15.1 billion) into AI infrastructure in the country, marking the tech giant's largest single investment in Europe.

#145. Binance users withdraw $3.6bn after largest-ever crypto hack

Binance customers withdrew $3.6bn (Â£2.7bn) of assets from the world's biggest cryptocurrency exchange as it reopened on Sunday following what is believed to be the largest hack in history.

The firm suspended all transactions in the wake of the incident, which impacted $6.3bn worth of digital assets.

Major organisations, including the US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), have accused North Korea of being responsible for the cyber-attack.

The company said it has covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach but the remaining amount may take longer to source. Binance has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack.

Binance said on X: "Between 06:00 and 15:30 [GMT] on November 1, we processed $4.649 billion in withdrawals and $1.01 billion in deposits, resulting in a net outflow of approximately $3.64 billion."

There was an "initial spike" in withdrawals, which later declined and "are stabilising as expected", it added.

Sunday's net withdrawals account for just over 2% of the $171bn of assets under its management, said Binance.

The firm said last week that it has reserved more than $6.3bn in funds, to deal with âthe fallout of the attack.

Binance also said withdrawals of more than two dozen major cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin and Ethereum have resumed and that more services will resume in phases once security checks are completed.

Funds linked to the Binance hack continue to be laundered, according to Alec Zebrick, senior manager in global services at Chainalysis, which has been working with the firm and governments to get the stolen money back.

"We continue to see funds being laundered quite actively," he told the BBC's Today Programme.

"The attackers are layering these funds across many thousands of addresses, across thousands of deposits," he added.

AI discovered a massive cyber-security flaw - here's what happened

The attack on Binance has been attributed to TraderTraitor, a North Korean cybercrime group also known as the Lazarus Group, which stole a then-record $1.5bn from crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

The FBI on Saturday published a list of cryptocurrency wallet addresses linked to the group and urged partners in the industry to help by blocking transactions involving them.

The exchange scrambled to urge cryptocurrency issuers to freeze affected assets.

Binance said last week that early checks suggest the incident may have started with a compromised laptop that belonged to an engineer, allowing hackers to find a way to make unauthorised transactions.

The hack hit Binance's global centralised exchange, Binance.com, while its US-facing platform has said it was not affected by the issue.

Watch: What we know about Binance's $6.3bn hack and what to do if you're affected

Binance said it had replaced customers' assets using a combination of sources, including its corporate treasury as well as its futures insurance and emergency reserve funds.

"Our commitment remains the same: Binance will fully cover the financial impact of the funds stolen in this incident," the firm said.

Cryptocurrencies are digital assets traded on exchanges such as Binance. Unlike cash, they do not exist in physical form and are managed on computers, smartphones and other digital devices.

Binance was founded in China and has hundreds of millions of registered users globally.

#144. Poundland's former owner offers to invest as minister prepares to meet board

Poundland has been given until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Poundland's founder has said he would invest in the firm and a government minister said she would meet the firm's board amid its administration fears.

Steve Smith, who set-up the Walsall-based business, said he had attempted to contact owners Gordon Brothers having been "really surprised" to learn about them filing for administration when sales were up 11% on last year.

The BBC understands that Business Minister Kate Dearden will meet with senior Poundland executives later this week to discuss the company's threatened insolvency.

US-based Gordon Brothers said it was evaluating interest for the whole business and formal and indicative offers for significant viable parts of it.

Gordon Brothers, who bought the firm from Polish retail group Pepco last year for Â£1, has stopped funding the business and rejected a takeover offer from Irish firm Martin Property Group (MPG).

The retailer has given the business until Thursday to find a rescue deal, putting up to 12,000 staff at risk.

Steven Smith founded Poundland in 1990 with the help of his father, Kevin Smith

It's understood the meeting with Deardon will be predominantly a "fact finding" meeting with government sources conceding there is limited scope for the government to intervene in a commercial transaction albeit one that puts over 10,000 jobs and hundreds of stores at risk.

The meeting was first reported by Sky News.

There is no current meeting planned between the government and executives from current owners, US private equity group Gordon Brothers.

Concerns have been raised that Gordon Brothers have declined an offer from another buyer to keep Poundland afloat in order to ensure repayment of Â£30 million loan it inherited after buying the company for Â£1.

'Poundland closure would leave a void in our town'

How Poundland grew from a market stall to a high street giant

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has said the government was "ready to support" staff as he also called for answers over why an offer to buy the business had been rejected.

Smith, from Wolverhampton and who set up the firm in 1990 with a store in Burton-upon-Trent, Staffordshire, said he contacted the owners over the last three months but had no reply. He had thought that was good news as he assumed the firm must have had offers on the table.

"And maybe I was a bit too late, but I'd certainly... with talking to the staff and how Poundland's performing at the moment, I'd certainly, if there's an opportunity, I definitely want to put some money back in there."

He said he was still in touch with staff there at levels within the business and "even last week" sales were 11.2 % upon last year.

"Although Poundland made a loss last year, they're going to make a good profit this year, so it's just crazy."

Staff held a protest outside Poundland's Walsall-based HQ last week

He warned that the two or three thousand suppliers to the firm only worked for Poundland so they would be directly affected by its collapse.

"This is not just about 12, 000 jobs. I'm estimating more like 20 or 30,000 jobs," he added.

The company has 600 stores in the UK and Ireland, and Smith said it had between four and five million customers a week, so with Poundland not being present on high streets would "cause a lot of other retailers to fall as well".

Liam Byrne, chair of the Business, Innovation, Science and Trade Committee and Labour MP for Birmingham Hodge Hill and Solihull North, said he felt the company could be saved and "the whole deal stinks to high heaven right now".

"Because I think the issue that we've got is, you've got an owner that could well be trying to cut and run, to sell a business that is in fact a going concern in order to maximize their own profits and put in jeopardy 12,000 British jobs on the high street."

"Our message today to government and to ministers is, do not leave this to the last minute, which is Thursday... please get on the case now to get everybody around the table to maximize every option to save as much of the business as possible," he added.

In a statement, the board of Poundland Limited said it had been "working on a formal sale process for Poundland" and despite "enormous efforts it had not been possible to identify an acceptable offer for the business as a whole".

"We recognise any sale process creates uncertainty and particularly when set against a challenging UK economic environment."

Gordon Brothers said: "While Martin Property Group had been identified as the preferred bidder by Poundland's management team, attention must now turn to other active bids."

The interest, formal and indicative offers were being evaluated "with a view to minimising uncertainty for Poundland's people and all relevant stakeholders."

#143. Blackstone president calls for more equity to fund AI buildout as debt risks mount

The artificial intelligence infrastructure buildout will need more equity financing as lenders become increasingly exposed to the companies driving the buildout, Blackstone President Jon Gray said Tuesday.

Speaking at the Hong Kong Monetary Authority’s Global Financial Leaders’ Investment Summit, Gray said the scale of capital required for AI is creating concentration risks for debt investors, both across the sector and at individual companies.

Unlike shareholders, who can benefit from a company's growth, lenders have limited upside as their returns are largely confined to interest and principal repayments, he said.

Speaking at the same panel, Pimco CEO Emmanuel Roman warned investors that the scale of AI financing could prove to be "too much" for markets to absorb.

Roman said AI-related capital spending could reach around $10 trillion over the next five years, citing General Atlantic CEO Bill Ford's estimate. He added that about $550 billion in AI debt had been issued in various forms so far this year.

Even with a mix of equity and debt financing, the amount required would be “gigantic,” Roman said, comparing it with a global corporate bond market of roughly $20 trillion.

“At some point in time, it’s going to be too much,” he said.

Global data center demand will require about $8.5 trillion in capital spending over the next five years, Goldman Sachs wrote in a report Monday.

Gray said financing the buildout would require a combination of public markets, banks and private capital, with companies increasingly turning to structured transactions to limit the amount of debt they take on. Cash generated by hyperscalers would also fund part of the spending, he said.

“The creativity is needed because the scale of capital is enormous," Gray said.

Despite those financing challenges, Gray remained bullish on AI infrastructure, saying demand for computing power was “running well ahead of supply.”

Spending on AI models across Blackstone’s portfolio companies had risen 20-fold over the past year as businesses saw financial benefits from uses including coding, customer engagement and content creation, he said.

Meeting that demand remains difficult, however, as securing sites, permits, chips, power and capital becomes more challenging, according to Gray.

#142. Ryanair says it may freeze workers' pay and raise airfares

Ryanair is considering a temporary wage freeze for staff and confirmed airfares will be "materially higher" next summer because of a sharp rise in fuel costs following the US-Israel war with Iran.

The airline said it expects fuel prices to remain high "well into 2027" unless there is a "swift resolution" to the conflict.

Ryanair said its fuel bill is forecast to be â¬2bn (Â£1.6bn) higher in the short-term and its boss, Michael O'Leary, said airfares would have to rise by "double-digits" to help cover the cost.

But the firm said that was unlikely to be enough and it is looking into pay freezes. Unite, the union representing Ryanair staff, said it had not been told of "any such proposals".

O'Leary also said the company would slow down hiring as airline's costs rise.

The airline said its pre-tax profits fell by 16% to â¬2.4bn during the six months to the end of September, largely due to fuel costs jumping by 15%.

The company said that although 80% of its fuel bill had been "hedged" â in effect, bought in advance at fixed prices â the cost of the remainder was elevated.

O'Leary reiterated forecasts from earlier this year Ryanair would "need a 20% fare increase" to cover the cost of jet fuel.

"I'm not sure we'll get to 20%," he said. "But we're certainly looking at double-digit fare increases across Europe from all airlines in 2027."

Aviation fuel prices have surged this year as a result of the US-Israel war with Iran, which has dramatically reduced the supply of refined products coming from the Gulf and increased competition for those produced elsewhere.

Ryanair also said it was weighing up whether to freeze pay, as multi-year wage deals with unions come to to an end. The policy could be implemented from April next year.

Oliver Richardson, national officer at Unite, said the union "hasn't been advised of any such proposals and we have a multi-year pay deal in place which covers next year. A number of Unions across Europe have the same".

He said: "Our pay agreement was not attached to, nor dependent on company performance. This was something that the Ryanair was at pains to emphasise. As such we would expect it to be honoured."

Ryanair also said it would increase its use of artificial intelligence within its back office and support functions.

Meanwhile, Ryanair has cut back on winter flights and said on Monday there would be more to come. It said this was happening across the airline industry, particularly with short-haul carriers.

O'Leary said: "We believe those cuts will continue into the summer of 2027. Over the medium term, we expect short-haul capacity will remain constrained out to the early 2030s."

#139. Starbucks sales miss expectations, but its U.S. turnaround is gaining steam

Starbucks on Thursday reported mixed quarterly results as its international cafes underperformed expectations.

However, its U.S. turnaround continues to take hold, as its home market topped expectations during the coffee giant's final quarter of fiscal 2026.

"We enter fiscal 2027 with momentum, a clear set of priorities and higher ambitions for what Starbucks can become," CEO Brian Niccol said in a video shared on the company's website.

Starbucks is ahead of schedule to meet the fiscal 2028 targets laid out at its investor day in January, he said on the company's earnings conference call.

For fiscal 2027, Starbucks is projecting adjusted earnings per share in a range of $3.15 to $3.25, above Wall Street's expectations of earnings per share of $3.14. However, its anticipated revenue decline of 1% is weaker than expected; analysts were forecasting net sales growth of 1.4%.

Starbucks is projecting North American same-store sales growth of 4% for the fiscal year.

The company's earnings and revenue outlook includes an additional week and the effects of the shift in its China business to a joint venture business model. Starbucks is also projecting slight deflation in coffee prices and slowing net new North American cafe openings, which will both weigh on its full-year revenue, according to CFO Cathy Smith.

Here's what the company reported for the quarter ended Sept. 27 compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Starbucks reported fiscal fourth-quarter net income attributable to the company of $677.2 million, or 59 cents per share, up from $133.1 million, or 12 cents per share, a year earlier.

Excluding restructuring and impairment costs and other items, the company reported earnings per share of 80 cents.

Net revenue fell 4% to $9.14 billion, reflecting the joint venture deal in China.

The company's global same-store sales increased 7.2%, fueled by growth in average ticket, and beating Wall Street estimates of 6%, according to StreetAccount. Transactions grew 1.8% in the quarter.

Starbucks' U.S. cafes reported same-store sales growth of 7.7%, topping StreetAccount estimates of a 6.5% increase.

Under Niccol's "Back to Starbucks" strategy, the company has focused on improving service, making its cafes more welcoming and sharpening its marketing. The changes have helped to win back customers.

"Starbucks brand affinity is at near record levels as our menu innovation, marketing and merchandise continue to create moments that drive culture and bring people into our coffee houses," Niccol told analysts.

The company said its North American rollout of its fall menu, which includes the iconic pumpkin spice latte, was the company's strongest ever autumn launch day. And matcha is now a $1 billion drink for Starbucks.

Outside of North America, the company's international comparable sales ticked up 0.9%, as traffic declines offset an increase to average ticket. That growth fell short of analyst estimates of a 3.2% increase, according to StreetAccount.

After its China business transitioned to a joint venture, Starbucks has shifted most of its international business to a licensed model, allowing local operators to take charge. The company is also exploring selling a majority stake in its Japan business, Reuters reported in September.

Starbucks opened 303 net new cafes during the quarter. In September, the company announced it would close about 1% of its North American footprint, or about 250 locations, as part of its turnaround.

By the end of its fiscal year, across the U.S. and Canada, the company also completed more than 1,700 cafe uplifts, meant to make its stores more welcoming to customers. The company has said locations that receive a makeover, with tweaks like increased seating and softer lighting, see higher transactions in the aftermath.

#138. Christmas 2026 stamps unveiled by Royal Mail

Two stamps from the Royal Mail's annual Christmas collection

Royal Mail has unveiled its Christmas stamps for this year - featuring scenes from the Nativity.

The series of five stamps have been jointly created by graphic designer Gareth Howat and illustrator Rebecca Sutherland.

The stamps depict familiar scenes of Mary and Joseph travelling, baby Jesus, an angel, the shepherds and the Three Wise Men.

This year's collection marks the 60th anniversary of the first-ever Christmas special stamp.

Sutherland said it was an "absolute honour" to be given the opportunity to illustrate the Christmas stamps for the Royal Mail.

She said she has loved stamps since she was a child and had even collected them. "Being asked to create a set myself feels incredibly special," she added.

The Christmas stamps from recent years have featured snowy scenes of UK cathedrals, or lyrics from famous carols - although 2025 also showed scenes from the Nativity.

The first Christmas stamp collection came out in December 1966 after a children's art competition was announced by the then-postmaster Tony Benn.

David Gold, of Royal Mail, said: "We hope this year's Christmas stamps will add an extra touch of festive magic to the millions of greetings sent to family and friends at home and around the world."

#137. Apple unsure when it will get more foldable iPhones ahead of Christmas

Chief executive John Ternus was presenting his first Apple results since taking over from Tim Cook

Apple has said it does not know when it will get more of its new foldable iPhones back in stock as the company heads towards the key Christmas trading period.

The iPhone Duo sold out just over a week after its launch, despite being Apple's most expensive smartphone at a cost of nearly $2,000.

The company warned of "constraints" in sourcing key components and admitted it was "really hard to predict when supply and demand will be in balance".

Meanwhile, Apple's new chief executive John Ternus said the firm was seeing an "unprecedented" price rise in memory costs, including chips, as the artificial intelligence (AI) industry continues to invest billions of dollars in building data centres.

Ternus launched the Duo after he took over from previous boss Tim Cook in September.

It is the smartphone's first major design change in almost 20 years but some buyers have reported problems with the Duo's screen.

Last week, the company said it would replace any handsets that were affected by an issue that caused a "small number" to crack along the inside fold.

As it released its latest financial figures on Monday, Apple's executives did not mention any problems with the iPhone Duo.

Ternus said he was "thrilled" by the early reaction to the foldable phone. Chief operating officer Sabih Khan said the company was working hard to get the Duo "into the hands of our customers as quickly as possible".

Apple's results for the three months to 26 September did not include sales of the Duo, which was released at the end of October.

Nevertheless, Apple said it had sold more iPhones globally over the last year than ever before.

iPhone revenue for the quarter rose 17% to $57bn (Â£42bn).

Sales of Mac computers also grew over the three months by 27% to a record $10.7bn.

Overall, Apple's sales between July and September rose from $102bn to $114bn. Pre-tax profit also increased, from $32.8bn to $36.1bn.

However, sales for both the iPad and Apple's wearable products, including its headphones and watch, fell by 10% and 7%, respectively.

Khan said that most Apple devices were still experiencing supply constraints driven in part by difficulty in getting enough microprocessors with advanced nodes.

Those components are essentially computer chip technology that allow Apple devices to operate more quickly, most of which it has manufactured through TSMC, which is based in Taiwan.

Khan said the problem was expected to continue into next year, particularly impacting iPads and Macs.

The situation has been made worse by what the company has repeatedly insisted is "unexpected demand" for its products.

Ternus noted, too, that Apple is still seeing "unprecedented" cost increases for memory, or the ability of devices to store data like apps, photos and text messages.

Much memory storage runs through data centres, which have experienced a boom in demand for space as AI chatbots and platforms - including those developed by the likes of OpenAI and Anthropic - have become popular.

Watch: Would you buy Apple's $2,000 foldable phone?

Storage prices have gone up for tech companies like Apple.

Earlier this year, Cook referred to a sudden increase in data centre costs as a "thousand year flood".

Apple has lifted prices on nearly all of its hardware products by around 20%.

Sunil Krishnan from Aviva Investors told the BBC's Today programme that Apple posted "a reasonably solid set of results" with a "pretty good performance" from its iPhone business.

While there were concerns over the company's guidance on profit margins and the cost of memory chips, Krishnan added that in the broader context, "margins well north of 45% for a very well-established company, I think, are pretty impressive".

However, Apple's share price fell by 1.8% in after-hours trading.

#135. From scooters to shampoo - the people saving hundreds from dumpster diving

Darryl Baker was shocked to see piles of clothes dumped beside the recycling bins outside her local supermarket.

On several trips to the dumpsters she has also found a host of other items, such as "a perfectly decent" Ikea picture frame and children's scooters.

Always on the lookout for ways to save money and appalled by the waste, the thrifty mother-of-two from north London asked supermarket staff if she could take some of it.

Permission granted, "I got my children entire new wardrobes," says Darryl, 39, adding that she's stuff put away for years to come.

During a six-month stint this year, "I have probably saved hundreds of pounds," says the freelance journalist.

Darryl reels off the names of brands she has salvaged - Ralph Lauren, Nike and The North Face, among others. "My mind was blown," she says. "This stuff is just strewn all over the floor."

Darryl Baker takes the clothes for herself and her children, and to sell

In addition to finding clothing for her children and her nephews and nieces, she keeps some for herself. Others she donates or sells, including recently getting Â£30 for a men's Barbour jacket.

Darryl has joined the ranks of so-called dumpster divers â people who rummage through commercial bins for treasures. Motivations include saving money, giving items to charity and disgust at how often perfectly usable items are being consigned to waste - or a combination of all three.

Texan Jeff Ferrell has been rooting around in trash for more than 50 years.

"I still dumpster dive every day by bicycle," says the 72-year old, who lives in Fort Worth, and has written books on the topic. "I still enjoy it as much, or more, than ever."

Jeff considers himself a "bit of Robin Hood mixed with anarchist direct action", only scrounging in more affluent neighbourhoods and giving away many of his finds.

"Almost everything in our home is scrounged, and I never buy, for example, soap or toiletries or medical supplies, or shoes and clothes and tools and garden supplies," he says.

Jeff Ferrell sells some of what he finds, and gives away other items

Jeff looks for discarded metal to sell at a scrapyard which, he says, earns him around $150 (Â£110) every two weeks. His wife sells his other finds, such as "an antique or piece of jewellery", on eBay.

Through savings and selling the more valuable items, he estimates his finds are worth "tens of thousands of dollars a year".

While dumpster diving may have been considered taboo in the past, it's now something many people are proud to do and post about online.

Millie Diamond, a London-based stylist, posts videos on Instagram of her rummaging through bins. But she has a very specific aim - to unearth fabrics and clothing that she can salvage and refashion into new items, some of which she then sells.

Millie Diamond turns dumped textiles and clothes into new outfits

She estimates that one dress she found was worth Â£600.

"I'm a bit of a magpie. I like collecting things off the street or anywhere that there's a freebie. If I see something that catches my eye, then I'll grab it."

Millie, 36, began bin diving after spotting some fabrics discarded by a business that had shut down. "I literally clambered into the bin. It turned out it was loads of vintage clothes."

For Charlotte Bayford in Essex it's all about timing. Her best finds come when students at Essex University head home for the summer and ditch anything they don't want to take with them.

This includes half-filled toiletries. "I haven't bought shampoo for eight years," says the 44-year-old sales assistant. "I'd say that has saved me some money."

Like other dumpster divers, Charlotte gives a lot of her finds to charity. But she has also filled her house with salvaged objects, and sells on some valuable items.

Food, as long as it remains sealed, is fair game too. "I've found tins of Heinz baked beans, perfectly fine. I'm quite happy to eat those," Charlotte says.

Charlotte Bayford gives some of her finds to charity

But is taking things out of bins and keeping them acceptable in the eyes of the law?

Jeff, who is professor emeritus of sociology at Texas Christian University, says many US cities have made the practice illegal, including New York, Cleveland and Wichita.

"Dumpster diving is considered illegal [in such places] because that which is thrown away now belongs to the city... or to the private company that provided trash services," he explains. Any dumpster diver also risks trespass charges.

In Fort Worth, where Jeff lives, items can only be removed from bins if they are on public land, external.

In the UK, Sunjay Versani of Duncan Lewis Solicitors says it depends on the circumstances.

"In many cases involving genuinely discarded goods, prosecution is unlikely," he says.

"But there is no general rule that something is free to take simply because it has been put in a skip or bin. If in doubt, the safest approach is to ask the owner or business for permission."

#132. North Korea denies being behind record $6.3bn Binance hack

Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korea's leader Kim Jong Un

North Korea has denied claims that it was behind the record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) hack of cryptocurrency exchange Binance, calling the allegations "absurd rhetoric".

Kim Yo Jong, a top Democratic People's Republic of Korea official and the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said on Tuesday that the accusations are another "sinister attempt" by the US to damage Pyongyang's public image.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI), the Secret Service and cyber analysts have said North Korea orchestrated the hack, which has been described as the largest in history.

This is the latest hack to be linked to North Korea, which has been accused of relying on cyber-crime to help finance its sanctioned economy.

Blockchain analysts and the FBI have said the attack was led by state-backed hacking group TraderTraitor.

The FBI has called on industry partners to help block transactions involving cryptocurrency accounts linked to the group.

"The US has made a trite provocation of linking it with the Democratic People's Republic of Korea again," Kim Yo-jong, the director of North Korea's General Affairs Department, said in a statement.

"It is a grave political provocation against the DPRK that the US misleads the international public opinion as if all the cyber crimes in different parts of the world are our deeds," she said.

Why North Korea's 'elite' hackers are the prime suspect in major Binance hack

Who could be behind the Binance hack and what we know so far

But Pyongyang has been blamed for a number of major cyber attacks in recent years - accusations that it has denied.

The TraderTraitor unit, which is also known as the Lazarus Group, was blamed for a then-record $1.5bn hack of crypto exchange Bybit in 2025.

Pyongyang has systematically built its cyber operations, recruiting promising students from universities and sending them to hacker units overseen by North Korea's intelligence agency, research groups like the S Rajaratnam School of International Studies think tank have said.

The country has consistently denied such claims.

Kim Yo Jong said "it is the consistent policy stand of the DPRK government to resolutely oppose all sorts of malicious acts in cyberspace".

Kim Yo-jong: North Korea's heir apparent?

Who is Kim Yo-jong? Video, 00:02:32Who is Kim Yo-jong?

Binance did not respond to the BBC's queries about North Korea's alleged involvement in the hack.

Since the incident, the crypto giant has largely focused its public statements on the technical nature of the breach and its response to protect users.

The company's spokesperson said that a net $4.72bn has been withdrawn from the exchange since transactions resumed on Sunday.

The net outflow represents a "relatively limited portion" of Binance's total assets, which stand at approximately $157bn, the spokesperson told the BBC on Tuesday.

That figure reflects the impact of the hack, subsequent withdrawals and market price changes.

"Withdrawal levels are stabilising to what we would consider normal and expected on-chain activity," she added.

The company said last week that initial checks indicated that an engineer's laptop had been compromised, enabling an attacker to make unauthorised withdrawals.

Trading in major cryptocurrencies on Binance has since resumed after the exchange worked with issuers to freeze assets impacted by the hack.

The company has repeatedly said it would cover all of the losses linked to the attack and that it has so far covered around 95% of the assets stolen in the breach.

#130. Trump is going to get 'trounced' in the midterms — and knows it, Anthony Scaramucci tells CNBC

U.S. President Donald Trump will get "trounced" in Tuesday's midterm elections, former White House communications director and SkyBridge Capital founder Anthony Scaramucci told CNBC.

Speaking to CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Tuesday, Scaramucci said America is in the throes of a cultural and economic struggle, with the two main parties fighting to deliver "two different visions of where the country should go."

"I think the president's going to get trounced, by the way, and I think he knows he's going to get trounced," he said.

Trump's economic approval rating hit an all-time low last month, according to CNBC's All-America Economic Survey, with 65% of respondents disapproving of the president's handling of the economy, while 80% rated the economy as fair or poor.

"You have one vote. You have one customer relations survey that you can do on the American president — that's at the midterms, and people are not happy," Scaramucci added.

"He promised affordability, he promised no more forever wars, he promised transparency on the Epstein files," Scaramucci said on Tuesday. "He didn't deliver on any of that stuff, and so there's a lot of people inside MAGA that are now also breaking from him."

The White House did not immediately respond to a CNBC request to comment. The White House previously told CNBC that Scaramucci's "relevance ended almost a decade ago." Scaramucci was White House communications director for 10 days in 2017, during Trump's first term.

Prediction markets are now pricing in around a three-in-four chance of the Democrats winning control of the Senate in Tuesday's election. Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

On the Iran war, Scaramucci said he was "bearish" on a potential diplomatic breakthrough after the ballot.

"I don't see any alignment between the Iranians, the Israelis, and the Americans for a potential negotiated solution right now," he said. "If the Americans are going to ramp up the military strikes alongside of the Israelis ... I don't really understand how that's going to necessarily help the region either."

The U.S. launched "defensive" strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island overnight, a U.S. official told MS Now. Trump previously said American forces would not attack Iran before the midterms.

"Wars are very bad for business," Scaramucci told CNBC Tuesday. "They're not great for the stock market. So I would like to have a negotiated settlement, but there's nothing on the matrix where you see the concentric circles lining up, where these three groups would say, 'okay, that's a deal that all three of us are willing to accept.'"

Scaramucci also said "disarray" within the Democratic Party had been "the best thing that has happened for Donald Trump."

"And so, if he gets trounced here, there will be bicameral investigations that will be the main show because there's a lot of things that's going on in the administration that the Democrats are going to want to uncover," he said.

"The Republicans, in my mind, I think there'll be a great intellectual civil war. The Republicans are going to have to reconcile where do they want to go. It's no longer the party of Ronald Reagan, certainly not the party of George W. Bush, but is it the party of Donald Trump after he leaves the stage?"

Scaramucci added that it was going to take a long while to get away from the so-called MAGA movement.

"There'll be DNA elements of MAGA in the next presidential candidate for sure, because looming large over that presidential candidate will be Donald Trump and several hundred million dollars of his PAC money, and so they're not going to want to get too far away from him in 2028. So there will be elements of MAGA, but I also think there'll be a civil war in that party," he said.

#128. Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack

'All this food is now useless': Piles of rubbish after shop freezers hit by cyber-attack

Dozens of supermarkets across seven European countries have been hit by a cyber-attack affecting their refrigerators and freezers.

Around 40 Delhaize supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Sabcobel, a Belgian company that manufactures some of the affected refrigerators, said the units' evaporators were switched to defrost mode, causing them to warm up, in what is believed to be the same method used in last month's incident that hit hundreds of French supermarkets.

Millions of euros worth of food had to be thrown away in October, local media reported at the time.

Freezers have had to be emptied at several ICA grocery stores in Sweden after the cyber-attack

Stephan Moelans, the CEO of Sabcobel, which operates Delhaize's systems, on Monday told the Belga news agency that the incident was a cyber-attack and that the company had been "working all night and morning to disconnect the affected units from the network".

He said since the French attack in early October, the company had been working to "protect our customers fridges against this vulnerability" but that the units needed physical modifications, not just software updates.

Most supermarkets across Europe are linked to the internet, and maintenance and troubleshooting can be done remotely.

"The simplest solution is to manage the fridges over a secure internet connection. Most of our customers switched to that after the attack in France," Moelans added.

The temperature of freezers in several shops across Europe reportedly rose to as high as 50C (122F).

It is unclear who is behind the latest apparent attack.

In the US, authorities are investigating what caused refrigeration malfunctions at a number of American military bases in August and working together with French officials to examine whether the incidents could be linked.

Both Belgium and Sweden's national cyber-security agencies said they were monitoring the incident.

The UK's National Cyber Security Centre said it was working with international partners and industry to "fully understand details of an incident". No incidents have been reported in the UK.

In Flanders, northern Belgium, a Delhaize branch in Hoeilaart told news website Het Laatste Nieuws it had lost â¬100,000 (Â£83,500) worth of produce.

The manager said that estimate did not even take into account further losses that would result from closing the shop.

Carrefour, a French-owned supermarket, said the incident had only affected 15 of its Belgian stores, adding that measures had been taken immediately to ensure all shops remained open.

A spokesperson for Colruyt Group, which operates Spar stores in Belgium under a franchise system, told Reuters news agency five shops had their fridge systems attacked.

Sweden's budget furniture retailer Ikea said refrigeration systems of its store in the western Belgian city of Mons were affected, with a spokesman quoted by Reuters as saying "we cannot rule out that additional locations may be affected".

An employee at an ICA supermarket in Sweden's capital Stockholm was pictured throwing away bags of spoiled food

In Sweden, several ICA and Coop shops were hit.

A spokesperson for ICA confirmed the incident had affected a "small number of ICA stores operating outside ICA's central network".

"ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the situation," they said.

One of the ICA branches located in SjÃ¶bo, southern Sweden, wrote on social media: "Our refrigerators and freezers have been hacked overnight and they have set the temperature to 50 degrees."

The store was closed earlier but has now reopened to customers.

Seven out of the 800 Coop stores in Sweden were also affected but the "situation is currently contained and under control", the company told the BBC.

In neighbouring Finland, refrigerator malfunctions were detected in some K-stores.

The owner of the supermarket chain Kesko was quoted by Finland's Helsingin Sanomat newspaper as saying its security experts were investigating what caused the problem.

The attack is also affecting shops in Ireland, the Netherlands and Estonia.

In Ireland, it impacted 13 SuperValu supermarkets. A spokesman for the owner of the chain, Musgrave Group, confirmed to The Irish Times that a "cyber incident" affected its refrigeration systems, but added that the impact was limited to a "specific model" of refrigerator.

A spokesperson for Danish manufacturer Danfoss told Dutch public radio channel NOS that the freezing and cooling systems of at least 17 companies in the Netherlands were attacked overnight, affecting butchers' shops and small neighbourhood stores.

A Selver supermarket in Estonia was also affected and there are reports of other affected companies in the country, according to ERR.

#127. Cheaper cars are selling considerably faster than at the beginning of the year

Consumers are concerned about rising fuel prices and affordability — and that could be showing up in how fast the least expensive cars are moving off dealer lots.

From the first quarter to the third, the number of days it took a dealer to sell a vehicle priced between $20,000 and $29,999 dropped 20%, according to data from Edmunds. That pace of selling saw the biggest acceleration of any price segment.

Vehicles priced from $30,000 to $39,999 sold about 18% faster, and the number of days it took to sell the next tier — $40,000 to $49,999 — dropped nearly 12%, according to Edmunds.

All of those price segments also took fewer days to turn than their more expensive counterparts. The lower-priced tiers took under 60 days to move off lots, with the least expensive ones selling the fastest. Every segment of vehicles above $50,000, meanwhile, took more than 60 days to sell.

"If you're a dealer and you have inventory and that price point, you're definitely noticing," said Edmunds Director of Insights Ivan Drury.

Cheaper vehicles may be selling faster, but they're still hard to find. The average listing price for a new vehicle in September was $49,726, according to Cox Automotive.

And, the overall sales numbers for some key segments priced under $40,000 have fallen. This has especially been true for compact cars, which are down roughly 4% since the beginning of 2026, and compact SUVs, down nearly 8% in the same period, according to Cox Automotive.

These different data points seem to be moving in opposite directions. On the one hand, cheaper cars are moving faster off dealer lots. On the other, sales of affordable segments are down.

There are a few reasons for this, according to analysts who follow the industry. One is that there might not be enough lower-priced vehicles on dealer lots in the first place.

"I think there are some supply things going on at that end of the market," said Cox Automotive Deputy Chief Economist Mark Strand. "We have to kind of grade the market on a curve until we see what's going on with fourth-quarter data."

The compact SUV segment, in particular, is suffering from the lack of two popular models.

The Toyota RAV4, which is the most popular SUV in the U.S., has been in short supply at many dealerships this year, as the automaker shifts production to its Kentucky factory, Strand said. The company also has shifted the entire lineup to a hybrid powertrain at a time when hybrids have been in exceptionally high demand.

The other missing SUV is the Ford Escape, which that automaker discontinued to make way for its upcoming midsize EV pickup truck.

The other slow-selling, low-priced segment — compact cars — faces structural inventory challenges, said AutoForecast Solutions Vice President of Global Forecasting Sam Fiorani.

"We've seen the disappearance of affordable, compact new vehicles over the last decade," he said.

Detroit automakers swapped out their lineups of compact coupes and sedans in favor of small crossover SUVs, which tend to carry slightly higher sticker prices than comparably sized passenger cars.

Currently, there is not a single compact passenger sedan for sale by an American manufacturer. Most on the market are Japanese or Korean, with a few German models, such as the Volkswagen Jetta and the Golf hatchback.

"You have all these brands that have just abandoned the marketplace," Fiorani said.

The smallest American passenger cars are the Ford Mustang, General Motors' Chevrolet Corvette, and the midsize Tesla Model 3 sedan, according to AutoForecast Solutions. The firm classifies both the Mustang and the Corvette as sports cars, and as such they occupy a different niche than compact sedans like the Honda Civic or Toyota Corolla.

The Mustang has a starting price of $33,490, the Model 3 starts at $34,990 and the Chevrolet Corvette starts at $71,300. Cars like the Civic, Corolla and Jetta, meanwhile, all start below $25,000.

"Compact vehicle buyers are very price-sensitive," Fiorani said. "And so when the price of gas goes up, that eats into their car payment. That means that they can't afford a $500 or $600 a month car payment, and they have to move into the used market."

Indeed, the wholesale used prices of compact cars have risen and are up more than almost any other segment, according to Cox Automotive. Wholesale prices — the prices vehicles sell for at large auctions, like Cox Automotive's Manheim auction — are indicators for prices overall in the fragmented used-car market.

Along with the rise in prices, auto finance stats show that a historically high number of buyers are stretching to afford cars.

Roughly one in four buyers financing their purchases are getting 84-month loans — a record. Nearly 3 in 10 trade-ins toward new-vehicle purchases carried negative equity in the third quarter, meaning the cars being traded in were worth less than the outstanding balances of the loans on them. Shoppers are often rolling these negative balances into new loans, which pushes up the new payment cost.

Automakers have also been more tightly aligning production with demand to avoid discounting vehicles. Incentives on average are about 6.6% of the average transaction price as of October, according to Cox Automotive. Leasing offers a way to get a car with a lower payment, but leasing rates are also down.

Vehicle trade-in data suggests that many buyers who are snapping up those fast-selling cheaper vehicles are either first-time buyers or are purchasing supplemental vehicles, Edmunds' Drury said.

The subcompact SUV segment is one lower-priced group that has seen sales rise in 2026. That is a segment that attracts a lot of first-time buyers. But this is still a relatively small share of the total car market, Fiorani said.

"It's not the mainstream vehicle that Americans want," he said. "It is the vehicle that entry-level buyers need to get to work, to get to school. ... It's people who don't have families yet."

Repeat buyers are less likely to want to step down into a compact car or SUV, Drury said.

"The moment you get into that other vehicle and it's a little bit smaller, it's not as comfortable, it doesn't fit all your stuff, all your kid's equipment for soccer practice, you're done," he said. "You're not giving up 360 cameras, ventilated seats, heated steering wheels, these niceties that you've become accustomed to. Automakers know that too. Dealers know that."

The truly cash-strapped are either snapping up the relatively few low-priced cars on dealer lots, moving to the used market, or holding on to their cars longer.

"The people who are trying to buy the smaller vehicles are really hurt by the way the economy is at the moment," Fiorani said. "So those people are trying to move into used vehicles or trying to maintain their current vehicle as long as they can. Automakers should be prepared for a softening in the marketplace."
