#126. A little-known biotech’s high-stakes cancer bet just paid off and its shares doubled

Danish biotech Genmab has seen its shares double in three months after a string of major cancer drug trial successes, giving fresh momentum to its push to take greater control of the medicines it develops and sells.

Shares in the roughly $35 billion company surged 48% last week, including a 29% jump in a single session after strong results for petosemtamab, its experimental head and neck cancer treatment. The announcement marked Genmab's third successful Phase 3 trial in October, following positive results for treatments targeting ovarian cancer and lymphoma.

After nearly three decades spent building Genmab into one of Europe's prominent biotechnology companies, CEO Jan van de Winkel told CNBC on Friday the breakthroughs marked "a completely new era."

Historically, Genmab made money from royalties from drugs it helped develop, including Johnson & Johnson's multiple myeloma treatment Darzalex and Novartis' Kesimpta. Van de Winkel said Johnson & Johnson is expected to generate $16.5 billion to $16.9 billion in Darzalex sales this year, with Genmab receiving royalties of up to 20%.

Genmab is now using that financial firepower to build a business that keeps a much larger share of the revenue from its own treatments.

The biggest market reaction followed Genmab's Oct. 28 announcement that petosemtamab, combined with Merck's Keytruda, reduced the risk of death by 50% compared with Keytruda alone in a Phase 3 trial of patients with recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer.

The combination also significantly improved response rates and showed no new safety signals, according to Genmab.

Van de Winkel told CNBC the combination "could potentially set a completely new standard" of care although he declined to say whether the survival data might hold up as the data mature, citing still unpublished full data.

Days earlier, Genmab reported that its medicine Rina-S reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 71% and the risk of death by 57% versus chemotherapy in a Phase 3 ovarian cancer trial.

Barclays analysts said the results exceeded investor expectations and suggested Rina-S could offer an alternative for patients ineligible for AbbVie's competing treatment Elahere, or whose cancer had progressed after receiving it. They cautioned, however, that cross-trial comparisons are limited.

A third late-stage October win came from Epkinly, co-developed with AbbVie, which reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 51% when combined with standard chemotherapy in previously untreated diffuse large B-cell lymphoma.

Analysts see significant commercial potential for petosemtamab, including beyond its initial target market.

Jefferies analysts said the drug could generate $3.5 billion in peak annual global sales treating recurrent or metastatic head and neck cancer, and estimated an expansion into locally advanced disease could add another $3.5 billion, an opportunity they believe investors have yet to fully appreciate.

Declining to give specific numbers, van de Winkel said he expects petosemtamab to become a "multi-billion dollar drug."

Genmab is due to report third-quarter earnings on Nov. 5.

Genmab has built its business around antibody-based medicines, including so-called bispecific antibodies such as petosemtamab, which can bind to two different targets on cancer cells simultaneously.

Petosemtamab targets the proteins EGFR and LGR5, and is designed to both disrupt signals that help cancer cells grow and to encourage the immune system to attack the tumor.

Van de Winkel said Genmab's expertise in antibodies and their interaction with the immune system has also helped it spot promising medicines developed by others. It acquired petosemtamab through an $8 billion purchase of Merus in 2025 and Rina-S through a $1.8 billion acquisition of ProfoundBio in 2024.

Those deals are also central to Genmab's changing business model.

It fully owns petosemtamab and Rina-S, while splitting the commercial economics of Epkinly equally with AbbVie, much larger shares than with Johnson & Johnson and Novartis.

"From now on I'm going to increasingly hold on to ownership of these new medicines," Van de Winkel said.

Genmab now has 15 Phase 3 trials across its three lead compounds.

"The model is beginning to be validated and click together," he said. "I think the market gets it at this point finally."

The Merus deal underscored the scale of the bet. Genmab borrowed $5.5 billion to help fund the purchase, relying on its royalty income as a financial backstop if petosemtamab failed.

Owning more of its medicines also means taking on more of the work of selling them. Van de Winkel said the company already has much of the necessary infrastructure in place, having built up commercial capabilities around existing treatments including Epkinly and Tivdak, co-developed with Pfizer.

The company is expanding its sales presence in Europe and seeking to reach more cancer patients through community healthcare, beyond specialist hospitals, in the U.S.

Van de Winkel said he wants Genmab's majority-owned medicines to reach around 500,000 patients by the 2030s, up from fewer than 30,000 today.

He also acknowledged potential interest from larger drugmakers, saying Genmab was likely "high up on the radar screen," but stressed that his ambition remained to build an independent European biotech.

Greater ownership, however, also means bearing more of the cost and risk of clinical trials, regulatory approvals and commercialization.

Van de Winkel acknowledged that geopolitical shifts and drug-pricing pressures could complicate expansion plans and change the order in which Genmab launches medicines in different markets.

#123. 'Quiet quitting' could be hitting the economy - here's why

The so-called "quiet quitting" trend where workers do the basic requirements of a job but refuse to do anything more might be hitting the economy, analysis has found.

Over the last decade, Britons have become less engaged with the jobs they do and are less willing to put in extra effort, according to a Resolution Foundation report.

The thinktank said this could partly help explain the slump in productivity since the pandemic.

"This is self-evidently bad for workers, but it also doesn't bode well for productivity and labour supply, both of which are vital for lifting living standards in the years ahead," said Hannah Slaughter, principal economist at the Resolution Foundation.

Quiet quitting was popularised on social media shortly after the pandemic.

Frustrated by high demands, low pay, and a squeeze in living standards, many online posts said workers should only do what their bosses asked and nothing more.

So you still show up for work, but no more helping out with additional tasks or checking emails outside work hours, instead focusing on work-life balance and "acting your wage".

Yet, far from being just a talking point on TikTok, the Resolution Foundation said the findings in its report were "consistent" with the idea of quiet quitting.

'I was working 60 hours a week so I quiet quit'

The percentage of those surveyed willing to go the extra mile to help their employer has sunk from 69% in 2015 to 55% in 2025, according to their survey of 4,656 adults.

Meanwhile, those who were not even "fairly" satisfied with their roles has sank to one-in-four, the lowest level on record.

In addition, workers are less likely than 10 years ago to say they have an interesting job, with 63% of those surveyed saying so, down from 74%.

"Workers today are indeed a little less committed to their employers, and a little less intrinsically drawn to work in general, than workers were a decade ago," the report added.

This shift over the last decade "may have contributed to weak productivity growth", which has slowed since the financial crisis and weakened again after the pandemic, the Resolution Foundation found.

Workers were also more focused on pay than in the past, which it said was "perhaps unsurprising... given the flatlining living standards of the past decade".

Sir John Curtice, politics professor and co-editor of the report, said the analysis does not mean that young people have "become more 'work-shy', nor have workers in general become more reluctant to change jobs".

"But if workers are to be fully engaged in their work, it has seemingly become more important for employers to provide jobs that are both financially rewarding and have flexible work hours," he added.

Are you quiet quitting? Tell us how and why.

#121. Apple shares slide after weak margin outlook; Palantir slumps 6% after earnings

Apple and Palantir shares fell in premarket trading Tuesday after both announced earnings after the bell on Monday.

The iPhone maker reported better-than-expected revenue in its fourth-quarter earnings, but fell short of estimates on iPad sales and issued a margin forecast that disappointed investors.

Palantir beat Wall Street's third-quarter estimates, with U.S. commercial revenue topping government for the first time.

Apple was last trading about 4% lower, and Palantir was down 6.5% in premarket trading.

Apple's statement marked the first earnings report since John Ternus took over as CEO from Tim Cook in September.

The company is reckoning with a global memory crunch and a scramble for computer components. Memory costs were higher this quarter than in the June period, the company said Monday. Despite the price surge, Ternus said Apple had just capped off its "best fiscal year ever."

He told CNBC's MacKenzie Sigalos that Apple's growth came despite supply constraints and foreign exchange headwinds.

"The concern is profitability," said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares. "Management pointed to memory as the main pressure, together with the cost of rolling out Siri AI. Memory shortages are also expected to limit Mac and iPad supply, as demand has run ahead of plans."

Apple shares have risen 25% in 2026, ahead of other big tech names, but that premium leaves "little room for disappointment," Todorova added.

Revenue nearly doubled from a year ago and blew past $2 billion for the first time ever, Palantir said in its earnings report. But the company also reported a slowdown in U.S. commercial total contract value, or TCV, bookings growth, said William Blair in a Tuesday note.

Investors will "no doubt be looking for evidence in subsequent quarters that 11% growth in U.S. commercial TCV bookings is a fluke of duration and not something structural," a Piper Sandler note from Monday said. "We believe that is the case and demand for PLTR's solutions still very much outstrip supply."

#120. UK and EU agree draft deal to scrap Brexit red tape over food exports

The UK and EU have agreed a draft deal on removing post-Brexit checks from food and agriculture exports, ahead of the UK-EU summit later this month.

The BBC understands the 200-page draft text for this so called "SPS" [sanitary and phytosanitary] agreement has been circulated to EU nations in Brussels for consideration at a meeting on Wednesday.

Similar texts for deals on emissions trading and a youth travel and work scheme have also been circulated alongside a 15-page draft joint statement for UK and EU top leaders.

The food deal is the most significant part of the so-called Brexit reset agreed so far, removing checks and red tape that hit exporters after the UK exited the EU's single market.

As a result of the agreement, the government claims the UK economy will eventually see Â£5.1bn worth of annual benefits.

The deal could have the following impact:

Exports to the EU of fresh sausages, burgers, shellfish from domestic waters and seed potatoes would be able to resume

It should remove the need for Â£200 Export Health Certificates, which confirm the product meets the health requirements of the country it is heading to

It should also end the need for Â£25 Phytosanitary certificates, which show that plants or plant products are free from pests and diseases

Inspector fees, organic certificates, some port fees and other checks for exports and imports to and from the EU could be removed

It will bring back passports for British pets being taken into the EU

In return, the UK is expected to commit to observing and adapting to existing EU regulations on plant, animal and food trade standards - a process called "dynamic alignment".

Each of the elements on the table was up for discussion under Sir Keir Starmer, and the summit would have taken place by now had Labour not replaced its leader in July.

Under Andy Burnham, Britain's approach to post-Brexit relations with the EU have shifted significantly. Sir Keir said the UK would not rejoin the bloc in his lifetime, while Burnham has said he would like to see Britain back in the EU in his.

Jon Walsh of Bio&Me said his firm had stopped exporting yoghurt due to vets' fees

Jon Walsh, the chief executive and co-founder of Bio&Me, which makes granolas, mueslis and yoghurts, told BBC 5 Live the draft deal sounded like "really good news".

"We're just a small company but when we were trying to export our yoghurts before, every time we'd send a pallet we had to pay a vet to inspect it and that was Â£140, and that meant that you'd lose money... so we had to stop."

The company also had problems exporting granola and overnight oats, with some shipments being returned.

"It was so frustrating because it was the same person filling out the same form and yet half would make it through and half wouldn't," Walsh said.

"You just kind of give up because you're losing money."

In an interview with ITV, Burnham said "public opinion has changed nationally" since the 2016 Brexit vote, but added he would "not run ahead of public opinion" on the issue.

"The public need to be the boss when it comes to whatever happens with Brexit," he added.

Burnham also said he had not proposed the return of freedom of movement between the UK and EU.

Asked by the BBC about the UK's relationship with the EU, Tory Leader Kemi Badenoch said that while we should "work closely", "dynamic alignment" with the EU would be a red line and "actually take us backwards".

Liberal Democrat Europe spokesperson Al Pinkerton said the draft deal was a "patch, not a fix".

"The real solution is to rejoin the single market and to establish a customs union with the EU," he added.

Reform UK's business spokesman Richard Tice said: "One of the key benefits of Brexit was that Britain no longer had to adhere to ridiculous EU rules.

"Returning to any form of alignment with Europe would crush Britain's competitiveness and force us to surrender sovereignty over trade policy, putting trade deals worth hundreds of billions of pounds at risk."

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen with Prime Minister Andy Burnham

While UK rules remain mostly aligned with EU ones, there are some areas where England has diverged from the EU rule book, for example on "precision breeding" of plants, previously known as gene editing. In recent weeks, agreement has been struck on transition arrangements while the EU's new regulations come in to force.

The government has already announced a European Partnerships Bill which will provide a legal mechanism for the application of new EU rules and regulations. This will also allow for the necessary alignments for the linking of the UK emissions trading scheme with the EU's, and save the need for UK exporters to pay the EU's new carbon border tax.

The draft agreements will be discussed at a meeting of EU ambassadors called Coreper on Wednesday, with some key details on the planned numbers in a Youth Experience Scheme to be finalised at a political level in the coming days. A discount on full foreign student fees for EU students is likely to be pushed to next year alongside a plan to reconnect electricity markets.

The BBC understands the draft agreements will also include some references to UK involvement in defence co-operation schemes, access to law enforcement databases and some reassurances over the EU's "Made in Europe" plans.

A food deal will have particular significance for Northern Ireland, radically reducing the impact of the post-Brexit Irish Sea border.

Northern Ireland's Brexit deal kept it inside the EU's single market for goods. That means food products from elsewhere in the UK require paperwork and some physical checks when arriving at NI's ports. A deal will ultimately end those requirements.

While the food trade plans have been backed by supermarkets and farmers representatives, some pro-Brexit campaigners have criticised the reapplication of EU rules. The post-Brexit trade deal signed by Boris Johnson's government prioritised regulatory freedoms for the UK over avoiding new regulatory red tape, checks and delays.

But William Bain, the British Chamber of Commerce's (BCC) head of trade policy, told the BBC's Today programme that business overall thought it was a "price is worth paying" to follow common rules, as companies would benefit from "lower costs, reduced delays [and] increased market access".

At the moment, he said, the requirement for Export Health Certificates can add thousands of pounds of costs to each truck carrying goods, in addition "to the delays that people find at the ports or at the borders".

"So we get lower exports because of the delays and the massive increases in costs that have come into the supply chain."

The BCC has released a new analysis of its members' views suggesting options for a closer relationship.

The business lobby group says the current trade relationship is "failing to deliver", leaving firms "struggling with a thicket of Brexit red tape" nearly six years after the UK's exit from the bloc.

#117. Drivers may get compensation offers before car finance saga ends

Drivers may be offered compensation from lenders ahead of the next legal battle in the car finance mis-selling saga.

Regulator the Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) said it would welcome lenders making settlement offers as long as customers were not given a worse deal than under its proposed central scheme.

That scheme is facing a legal challenge, to be heard in February, with resulting compensation unlikely to be paid until late next year.

But Barclays has confirmed it will make early compensation offers from now, with others expected to follow suit.

The FCA said it would still only expect a minority of claimants to be made early offers, with most having to wait for the outcome of next year's court case.

Stephen Braviner Roman, the FCA's general counsel and co-lead on the motor finance project, said: "Fair settlement offers could result in some people getting money sooner. It's clear they want the choice.

"Anyone who wants to wait can, and there's no obligation to agree to the offer. We'll closely monitor firms making offers and expect them to be clear what accepting an offer means."

The vast majority of new cars, and many second-hand ones, are bought with finance agreements. Customers pay an initial deposit to secure the vehicle, then a monthly fee with interest.

But millions of drivers who took out a car loan between April 2007 and November 2024 will be entitled to compensation after the loans were mis-sold.

They mostly related to when car dealers received commission from lenders, based on the interest rate charged to the customer. These were known as discretionary commission arrangements (DCAs) and customers were often not told about them.

Average compensation payouts of about Â£829 are expected to be made related to about 12 million car loans - about 37% of the total number during the period.

Car finance compensation payments delayed until next year

Compensation will cost the industry up to Â£9.1bn, including administrative costs. Individual payouts will vary, depending on the degree of harm each driver faced.

With huge compensation payouts inevitable, some lenders have already set aside millions of pounds to cover the cost.

Barclays has decided to start making compensation payments early, potentially to start clearing the cost off its balance sheet. It pointed out that claimants had already faced "a considerable wait".

"By making these early settlement offers, we are giving some customers the option of receiving redress sooner, rather than waiting for the legal challenges and any subsequent FCA action to conclude," said a Barclays Partner Finance spokesperson.

"Each offer will be calculated using the FCA's redress scheme methodology. We will clearly explain how it has been calculated and what accepting a full and final settlement means, so customers can decide whether accepting the offer is right for them."

The lender said claimants would start to be contacted directly "in the coming weeks".

But it stressed not all customers whose complaints were acknowledged on or before 1 October 2026 would receive an early settlement offer.

The FCA has published a factsheet to help drivers, external work out whether they should accept early settlements from lenders.

It will include a list of lenders who are making early offers, to ensure claimants with other lenders are not scammed.

The regulator said lenders will need to be clear with people about what accepting any offer means. This includes:

Being able to keep any payment, even if the final settlement under the central scheme after the legal challenges proved to be smaller

Claimants would not be able to claim more later if the outcome of the legal challenge meant they would have been paid more

Accepting an offer takes you out of the scheme and stops any further input from the financial ombudsman

The Finance and Leasing Association, which represents lenders, said: "We want to see consumers receive the fairest outcome in as timely a way as possible and recognise that, in some cases, that may point to early settlement, while in others it may be appropriate to await greater legal certainty."

But Kevin Durkin, solicitor advocate and director at HD Law, who represented Marcus Johnson who won his case in the Supreme Court, said he had "real reservations" that fair compensation would be offered.

"These are only heightened by reports that just a small number of claimants will receive what could be lowball offers. I fear this is crumbs from the table, for some customers who are highly likely to be owed more, while the vast majority will receive no offer at all," he said.

The main proposed FCA scheme remains partially suspended, pending legal challenges in February.

Consumer group Consumer Voice said the proposed compensation scheme left "too many people short-changed". Three lenders - Volkswagen Financial Services, Mercedes Benz Financial Services, and Credit Agricole Auto Finance - are also challenging the scheme.

The UK's Upper Tribunal has agreed to hear legal challenges to the scheme, with a hearing scheduled from 16 February to 3 March.

Anyone who is concerned about their car finance deal and has not yet complained can still complain directly to their lender. There is information on the FCA website on how to complain for free, external.

#115. New national home listing database is taking on Compass in the battle for control of real estate information

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Some heavy hitters in residential real estate just joined the fight over who can see residential property listings. Their goal, they say, is to counter private listings, which are becoming increasingly popular in certain markets.

AGNT (which owns eXp Realty and NextHome), HomeServices of America, Keller Williams and Real REMAX Group, four of the nation's largest brokerages, announced that they are creating a largely open and shared database (API) of listings for brokers — and inviting all other brokerages to join it. Those who do must submit a new listing within one business day. There is, however, an option within the system for certain private listings.

Open Real Estate is designed, according to its founders, to counter the increasingly fragmented residential listing market, and specifically private, off-market listings, which are favored by Compass, a brokerage behemoth. Compass is embroiled in more than half a dozen lawsuits with state and local Multiple Listing Services (MLS) involving listing rules and antitrust claims.

"We want to ensure that for the American consumer, there's a complete data set that is capital T truth, that's reliable as to what is happening in the marketplace," said Leo Pareja, CEO of AGNT. "So whether it's MLSs fragmenting, pocket listings, walled gardens, whatever, the goal is to ensure that there's a complete data set for the American consumer, so buyers and sellers have the most amount of accurate information."

Those who want to join the platform can do so with no initial charge or fees. Pareja said the four founders are going to make the investment into the infrastructure and the technology. Brown Harris Stevens, Lamacchia Realty, Samson Properties and Windermere are listed as initial participants.

"It's clearly a clap back to Compass, firstly," said Jonathan Miller, a longtime housing market analyst and director of markets at StreetMatrix, a housing data and analytics company. "And it's absolutely better because Compass is literally hiding listings from competitors by design."

Just after the announcement, Compass CEO Robert Reffkin posted a congratulatory message on X, saying that Compass is "excited that there will be a national real estate listing service that will return the power back to the creator of the listing — the homeowner and their trusted agent. This is good news for all agents and their clients, and we look forward to helping build the next chapter of listing services."

When asked, Reffkin declined to comment further.

Pareja's response to that: "We saw that, and we're grateful for his appreciation, and he's very much invited. We'd love to have him participate."

While Reffkin's response may seem odd, given that Compass supports private listings, there is one part of the Open Real Estate platform that does allow for exclusives. It's called "participant view." A property that goes into that view is put into a part of the database, "where only agents that are associated with participating brokerages can see it, and it cannot be publicly marketed by anyone, including the listing agent and the listing brokerage," according to Open Real Estate's website.

Reffkin's response came at the same time another MLS, New York's OneKey MLS, joined the California Regional Multiple Listing Service's federal lawsuit against Compass. The organizations are asking the court to firmly establish that rules protecting the shared, public marketing of listings are lawful and pro-competitive, according to a release. It followed OneKey's rejection of Compass's demands to dismantle local cooperation policies.

"This case raises questions that extend far beyond any one company or market," said Richard Haggerty, CEO of OneKey MLS in the release. "It goes to the core of how homes for sale are shared, how everyday buyers access that information, and how we protect a fair, equal housing market for everyone. We believe these issues demand clarity from the court, and joining CRMLS gives OneKey a direct, active voice in securing it."

Separately, last month six regional MLSs representing more than 120,000 real estate professionals nationwide filed a joint federal lawsuit against Compass seeking to uphold rules that promote access to listings through publicly shared databases.

Pareja said the new database is not meant to replace MLSs and that it should not be seen as a national MLS.

"We are going to work with MLSs. We're going to work alongside them. We're not usurping. We're not replacing," he said.

#112. Automakers like Rivian and Ford are ‘democratizing 3D printing’ to reduce costs and increase speed

DETROIT — Automakers including Ford Motor and Rivian Automotive are leaning into a new type of manufacturing line: 3D printing.

Manufacturers say it's helping them reduce costs and increase speed. The technology has been around for decades, but it's becoming more commoditized in the automotive industry, with 3D printers used to create everything from vehicle parts and accessories to tools for assembly plants and testing units for research and development labs.

"At this point, we don't have a choice but to scale up and begin using it through all factions of Rivian," Jonathan Dankenbring, Rivian senior manager of prototype manufacturing, told CNBC during a tour of the automaker's expansive 3D-printing operations in suburban Detroit. "We're going to be democratizing 3D printing for the entire company."

Also known as additive manufacturing, 3D printing can create three-dimensional objects from digital designs by dispensing material layer by layer, much like a traditional printer does with ink on paper. Instead of ink, the machines use a wide variety of materials such as plastics, composites, ceramics and even metals.

Printers can range from hundreds of dollars for hobby-like devices to hundreds of thousands of dollars for specialty equipment, depending on how big the product being produced is and the material it needs. They can produce individual pieces or hundreds of smaller parts in a batch using powders and resins.

Rivian said it has spent more than $5 million on 3D-printing machines, with the cost of materials for the operations growing to about $1.7 million annually.

Dankenbring said despite the costs, the machines typically pay for themselves within a year. He said the technology helps lower supplier tooling costs, streamline logistics expenses and cut production times for parts from weeks to days, if not hours.

"It's well in the millions [for savings], but the main thing is speed for Rivian," Dankenbring said.

The all-electric vehicle company's latest expansion of 3D printing includes adding new machines that can build flexible silicone parts as well as installing a bay of machines at its plant in Normal, Illinois, for employees to print parts on demand.

"3D prints should be readily available for every engineer so that they can make a part and get it in their hands as soon as possible," Dankenbring said.

Rivian has also used the technology for electric-motor models for fluid testing, creating screw guides for tooling and stamping press inserts and some production parts, including trim pieces to fill gaps in some early builds.

There's a similar push underway at larger, more-established automakers like Ford and its crosstown rival General Motors, which have used 3D printing for many years, including in their massive assembly plants.

Instead of having a bay of 3D-printing machines like at Rivian, Ford has departments for additive manufacturing at many of its plants to produce and distribute needed parts. That includes a new vending machine for 3D-printed parts for production of F-150 pickup trucks near its Dearborn, Michigan, headquarters.

"By putting the vending machine up, all the team leaders have access," said Gregory Foresi, a Ford mechanical engineer who ran Ford's 3D-printing lab at the plant from 2024 until last month. "We're able to keep that thing stocked rather than have a bunch of parts laying around in the lab. So that's been pretty huge."

Foresi used roughly two dozen machines to print off thousands of tools, some of which have been designed by employees, such as torque bit sockets, hood jackers and hand tools for smoothing adhesive.

Employees can use an app to request that specific auto parts or tools be printed. Ford's 3D-printing teams help develop them or determine what to produce next, as the machining time to build different parts can range from hours to days, if not weeks.

Foresi said creating the tools with 3D printing has allowed the automaker to uniquely design them when needed, get them faster and, in many cases, receive them at a lower cost.

Ford also has used 3D-printed parts to help keep production running. For example, Foresi said a large machining wheel was needed to maintain assembly line production and the company was able to 3D print the part until a permanent replacement from a supplier was available.

"They didn't perform as well, and it's not really a replacement for the ones that we were purchasing, but they kept the plant running," Foresi said.

Ford said it expects to have 3D labs deployed at more than 23 plants by the end of this year. Its current 3D-printing program has more than 200 machines, and there are additional printers outside the official program, the company said.

GM has used 3D-printed parts for some of its low-volume products in addition to research and development and inside plants, including recently expanding its use of industrial Stratasys printers that can cost hundreds of thousands of dollars based on use case, training and certification.

The Detroit automaker uses 135 components that are 3D-printed for its bespoke Cadillac Celestiq as well as other components for its performance Cadillac Blackwing sedans, including a unique medallion on the manual shifter knob.

Fadi Abro, senior global director of automotive and mobility at 3D-printing company Stratasys, said GM has been a great example of how companies can "standardize and then scale" 3D printing.

"The material properties improved so significantly that the use cases improved," he said, noting the company works with virtually every major automaker. "From a product perspective, we have to get the technology to become faster and more accessible, in an economic sense."

Correction: This story has been updated to reflect that Jonathan Dankenbring is Rivian's senior manager of prototype manufacturing. A previous version of this article misidentified him.

#110. Supermarket fridges hit with cyberattacks across Europe — here's what's happening

European supermarkets have become the center of another cyberattack, with hackers remotely heating fridges and spoiling food.

The attack caused temperatures in fridges and freezers to rise sharply, with at least one store reporting temperatures of up to 50°C (122°F) and other retailers discarding food. It's sparked concerns across the retail industry.

Dutch supermarket group Ahold Delhaize reportedly had dozens of Delhaize stores affected in Belgium and Luxembourg.

The Dutch supermarket giant told CNBC that there was an incident with refrigeration systems in dozens of its stores on Monday, which has resulted in food having to be discarded. It said the situation has since returned to normal with stores fully operational.

Meanwhile, Colruyt Group said five of its Spar stores in Belgium were targeted, according to Reuters. French supermarket Carrefour told the BBC that 15 of its Belgian-based stores were attacked too. France's Intermarché also said 15 stores out of 153 in total were impacted in Belgium.

"In most cases, the security measures in place made it possible to quickly contain the attack," an Intermarché spokesperson told CNBC. "However, a few stores did suffer some stock losses, although nothing comparable to the attack in mid-October.

"At this stage, the information available to us indicates that the bulk of the attack affected other players in the sector, notably Carrefour and Delhaize," they added.

IKEA told CNBC that a cyber incident affected refrigerators and freezers at its Mons store in Belgium between Sunday and Monday.

Although the store is still open to customers, the company is inspecting the affected equipment and products as well as checking installations across its other stores in Belgium.

"The incident has a limited impact on the offering in our restaurant and Swedish Food Market," an IKEA spokesperson said over email. "We are continuing our investigation to determine exactly what happened."

ICA, Sweden's biggest grocery retailer, also confirmed that it had been informed of an incident at a "limited number of stores," which were on networks outside ICA's central systems. "The incident has affected the stores' refrigeration and freezer systems. ICA is currently supporting the affected stores in managing the incident," the company said over email.

Elsewhere, Finnish Kesko supermarket, Irish grocery chain Supervalu and Estonian grocer One Selver also reported disruptions to their refrigeration systems.

Carrefour did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Hundreds of French supermarkets were targeted in October in a cyberattack that caused fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil.

Intermarché owner Les Mousquetaires told CNBC at the time that it was expecting damages of several million euros.

"Food security requires more attention at all levels because the weaponization of food has become a reality," European Commissioner for Agriculture and Food Christophe Hansen said on X in October.

Commissaries at six U.S. military bases also suffered freezer malfunctions in late August.

The Pentagon is now treating those as a suspected hack and coordinating with French authorities, four people familiar with the matter told Reuters in October.

In one case, the refrigerator's temperature was raised overnight, leaving masses of food melted and wilted by morning.

Sophos Counter Threats Unit's Senior Director of Threat Intelligence Rafe Pilling told CNBC that while there's no evidence AI has played a role in these attacks, elsewhere criminals have been using AI to speed up the scale and efficiency of attacks.

The attacks have yet to be claimed by any group or bad actor, and investigations are still underway, but it's raised concerns about the vulnerability of critical infrastructure.

Fredrik Blix, strategic cybersecurity advisor for Visente AB, said that the hack itself isn't sophisticated, as the control units for the refrigerators "sit directly on the public internet," with built-in support logins where the password can be obtained on the same day, according to information from researchers published in August.

"The vendor patched it in August," Blix said in a LinkedIn post. "Thousands of units never got the update, and the oldest models can't be updated at all."

He added that France was hit by the same attack three weeks ago, with France's CERT-FR telling Europe's operators to pull the devices off the internet. "The question I put to the suppliers and service companies who install and remotely manage this kit: what exactly did you do with those three weeks?"

Blix noted that the silence behind the attack is the most "interesting clue" as it rules out ransomware crews, leaving two options. Someone either carried out the attack simply because they can, or because someone wants to create "unease in Ukraine-supporting countries without leaving fingerprints," Blix said.

#108. Baked by Melissa enters Target with first at-home cupcake mix

Baked by Melissa will enter the at-home baking aisle in Target stores as the retailer tries to persuade shoppers to spend more at its locations this holiday season.

As of Sunday, Target is exclusively selling the brand's cupcake kits, liners and a cupcake pan, all in the dessert company's signature bite-size portions. The line includes three cupcake flavors: Christmas Tie-Dye, Marshmallow Hot Cocoa and Red Velvet.

The company's entry into at-home baking comes as a number of restaurants turn to consumer packaged goods to boost sales in an otherwise challenging time for the industry.

Some players, such as California Pizza Kitchen and TGI Fridays, have licensed their brands for frozen foods for decades. But other restaurant chains, including Chick-fil-A and Dutch Bros, have leaned into grocery products much more recently. For smaller companies, such as Momofuku or Carbone, space on a grocery shelf helps extend brand reach and gives consumers a chance to try their food without a reservation and at a much lower cost.

Founded by Melissa Ben-Ishay in 2008, Baked by Melissa has nine permanent locations in the New York area and ships its minicupcakes nationwide.

The regional chain has garnered attention outside the city thanks to Ben-Ishay's social media presence. On TikTok, the Baked by Melissa account has more than 2.8 million followers, fueled by popular recipe videos, such as for her green goddess salad dressing. Ben-Ishay's social media stardom has led to collaborations with Ferrero and Wonder.

Baked by Melissa had discussed at-home baking products for years, but Ben-Ishay, who is also president of the company, only decided to take the plunge a year ago during a trip to Target with her two kids, she told CNBC. A spin through the baking aisle made her decide Baked by Melissa "should be here," she said.

"I love baking cake mix. I truly do. And, quite frankly, sometimes I just think it tastes better," Ben-Ishay said.

But retail moves more slowly than Baked by Melissa's usual pace.

The dessert company needed just a few days to create New York Knicks-branded minicupcakes after the team won the NBA championship in June, for example, but figuring out retail products meant "a lot of waiting and approvals and new partners," Ben-Ishay said.

Twelve months later, the cupcake mixes have arrived on store shelves, just in time for the all-important holiday season.

For Target, the next two months will be critical as the retailer tries to woo back its core customer after a disappointing holiday season last year. That prototypical Target shopper often looks a lot like Ben-Ishay.

"We started with Target because I'm a 42-year-old mom of two, and I find myself there a lot," she said.

#107. Ferrari raises 2026 guidance after Q3 earnings beat

Ferrari on Tuesday raised its 2026 guidance after the sports car maker slightly beat Wall Street's third-quarter expectations.

Here's how Ferrari performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Ferrari raised its guidance to revenue of 7.65 billion euros, up from 7.6 billion euros; adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization to 3 billion euros, up from 2.97 billion euros; and adjusted EPS to at least 9.85 euros, up from at least 9.68 euros.

The automaker also upgraded its expectations for industrial free cash flow to up to 1.65 billion euros, up from previous expectations for at least 1.55 billion euros.

This is the second consecutive quarter Ferrari has raised its 2026 guidance, despite vehicle shipments being lower by 318 units through the first nine months of the year, at 10,170 vehicles, compared with last year. The decline is led by fewer shipments to the Americas and China, Hong Kong and Taiwan.

Ferrari said Tuesday that the guidance improvements were a result of stronger-than-expected customer personalizations, lower than anticipated currency headwinds and a second place finish in the Formula 1 championship.

"Our order book now reaches into 2028, and we still have two more launches to come this year. In addition, we continue to create engaging events around the world to further tighten the bonds with our clients and partners across Racing, Sports Cars and Lifestyle," Ferrari CEO Benedetto Vigna said in a release.

RBC Capital Markets analyst Tom Narayan said Tuesday that Ferrari's results were largely in line with Wall Street expectations. However, he said shares may be under "under slight pressure given some were hoping for a bigger guidance raise, but expect it to get better once folks realize these results are healthy."

Ferrari's U.S. shares initially declined on the results before turning positive and were up roughly 2% during premarket trading.

#104. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#101. Versant earnings beat expectations as digital platforms drive growth

Versant Media on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings that beat expectations and reaffirmed its full-year 2026 guidance, citing strength across its portfolio and growth in its platforms revenue.

The media company reported $245 million in revenue from digital platforms including Fandango, marking a nearly 10% year-over-year increase. Versant said Fandango saw "significant growth" in ticketing revenue in the third quarter, notching its highest level since the pandemic.

Advertising revenue, however, slipped to $373 million during the period, down 5.3% from last year, primarily due to the impact of last year's Ryder Cup, according to the company.

The Versant portfolio includes CNBC, MS NOW, Golf Channel and other cable and digital properties.

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter, compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Versant completed four major distribution renewals this year following its spinoff from Comcast's NBCUniversal.

"We're building deeper consumer relationships, expanding our reach and creating new opportunities for growth and monetization across our four core markets," CEO Mark Lazarus said in a statement.

During the third quarter, Versant said, its networks reached more than 120 million viewers monthly and its aggregate viewership grew by double digits year over year. The company also launched MS NOW's direct-to-consumer product and Fandango's on-demand product as new growth drivers. CNBC is expected to launch a direct-to-consumer product next year, Versant said.

For the period ended Sept. 30, Versant recorded net income of $47 million, or 34 cents per share, compared with $80 million, or 56 cents per share, the year prior.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $353 million, beating Wall Street estimates of $305 million, according to StreetAccount.

Revenue fell slightly to $1.59 billion from $1.66 billion in the year-ago period. The company said its revenue figure included the acquisition of golf simulation company Full Swing and the sale of sports management software company SportsEngine.

The company reaffirmed its full-year revenue outlook of between $6.2 billion and $6.45 billion and its adjusted EBITDA outlook of $1.9 billion to $2.05 billion. It adjusted part of its full-year guidance, saying it now expects free cash flow to exceed the prior range of between $1 billion and $1.2 billion.

Versant began trading as a standalone company on the Nasdaq in January after separating from Comcast. Though it began largely as a portfolio of cable networks, Versant executives have said they intend to diversify the company's revenue mix to eventually draw 50% from digital, platform, subscription, ad-supported and transactional businesses.

Live sports continues to be a major driver for the company. Versant said it saw "robust audience engagement" in the third quarter and that WNBA viewership on Versant's USA Sports networks was 25% higher than the prior year's cable average.

Versant also announced an additional $100 million accelerated stock repurchase program beginning Wednesday. It expects that transaction to be completed during the fourth quarter.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.

#98. Russia confirms new Ukraine peace talks as Melania Trump prepares to host ceasefire meeting

Russia on Tuesday confirmed that peace talks with Ukraine and the U.S. will go ahead next week in Abu Dhabi, while also signaling that a meeting between Presidents Vladimir Putin, Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to end the conflict was on the table.

Putin aide Vladimir ​Medinsky will serve ⁠as Moscow's chief negotiator ​at the talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, Kremlin spokesman ​Dmitry Peskov told reporters on a conference call, according to Reuters.

Separately, First Lady Melania Trump will bring Russian and Ukrainian representatives to the White House to discuss the local ceasefire in Kherson, the White House said Tuesday.

Monday saw the end of a six-day ceasefire in the Russian-occupied city of Oleshky and surrounding areas in the Kherson region to allow for evacuation of residents, in a pause of hostilities that the White House said was brokered by the first lady.

Ukraine on Monday accused Russia of using the ceasefire to reinforce its military positions in the city.

The first lady's summit on Thursday will be "an extraordinary moment in American history," the White House said, but did not name the representatives who will attend. Melania Trump will deliver remarks on the Oleshky ceasefire at 11 a.m. ET Thursday, it added.

Peskov also said Tuesday the Kremlin hopes to finalize plans for a trilateral meeting between the leaders of Russia, the U.S., and China in the coming days. The comments follow earlier reports that these countries were planning to negotiate an end to the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

Zelenskyy has said that any negotiations over Ukraine's future must include Kyiv.

"It can be acceptable only when we are at the table of negotiations," he said on Oct. 20, adding that he had discussed the proposal of a three-way meeting between the U.S., China, and Russia to negotiate an end to the nearly five-year full-scale war. "Nothing about Ukraine without Ukraine," he said.

Tensions between the U.S. and Ukraine have mounted over recent weeks amid continued strikes between Moscow and Kyiv, including on civilian and energy targets.

A series of moves by the Trump administration in October raised questions about Washington's reliability as Kyiv's most important military partner.

Tuesday's announcement follows U.S. special envoy Steve Witkoff saying late Monday that Washington will mediate Ukraine-Russia peace talks in Abu Dhabi on Nov. 10-11, focusing on security arrangements, de-escalation and humanitarian issues.

Witkoff also said that U.S. negotiators will meet European and NATO officials in Brussels this week to discuss post-war security guarantees and Ukraine's reconstruction.

#97. Yum Brands earnings beat estimates, but cyclospora outbreak weighs on Taco Bell

Yum Brands on Tuesday reported earnings that beat expectations, but a cyclospora outbreak linked to iceberg lettuce at Taco Bell weighed on the company's same-store sales growth.

However, Yum said Taco Bell, which the company considers a "growth engine" for its business, has since rebounded.

"Since the end of September, Taco Bell U.S. has been outpacing the [quick-service restaurant] industry on same-store sales growth," Yum CEO Chris Turner said in a statement.

KFC's same-store sales ticked up 1%, helped by system sales growth in China and Europe. The U.S., now the chicken chain's third-largest market, saw system sales shrink 6% in the quarter.

Like Restaurant Brands International's Popeyes, KFC has struggled in the U.S. amid a wave of new competitors and more budget-conscious consumers.

#93. Oil prices climb after U.S. strikes Iran's Qeshm Island

Oil prices climbed on Tuesday after the U.S. reportedly struck Iran's Qeshm Island late Monday, one day before the midterm elections.

Brent crude was up 1.75% on Tuesday at $106.60 per barrel, while West Texas Intermediate was up 1.7% to $98.10.

Oil prices rebounded a day after the U.S. and Iranian presidents signaled they are open to a diplomatic solution to the Iran war, which saw oil prices sell off over 7% on Monday.

But before this development, Brent prices were at their highest since 2022, as fears of further U.S. strikes on Iran after the midterms mounted.

Washington attacked Iran's Qeshm Island on Monday, U.S. officials told The Wall Street Journal, adding that the U.S. has been carrying out "limited strikes" against the country in recent weeks.

The military campaign against Iran has been kept deliberately low-profile to avoid signaling plans for a major escalation in fighting, the report said, quoting an unnamed official.

But despite these strikes, the Trump administration has also been touting a possible diplomatic solution to the conflict, with Vice President JD Vance telling CNN, "The president and I both feel really good about the prospect of potentially coming to a negotiated settlement."

On Sunday, U.S. Secretary of State Marco Rubio said President Donald Trump would like to resolve the Iranian war "diplomatically," and prefers not to use military force.

High oil prices have contributed substantially to inflation and high gasoline prices in the U.S., which have become a cause of concern for Trump ahead of the midterm elections.

Last week, two Federal Reserve presidents said interest rates will need to move higher to rein in inflation. Higher rates generally crimp growth and raise costs for consumers, but help to bring down inflation.

— CNBC's Priyanka Salve contributed to this report.

#89. Apple's John Ternus faces early test as global memory crisis hurts profits

John Ternus' first earnings report as Apple CEO was going fine until it was time to issue a forecast for the company's gross margin, one of the key metrics followed by investors.

Apple reported better-than-expected earnings and revenue on Monday, including in the company's core iPhone unit, and even projected sales in the current period that topped estimates. But just before Ternus opened the earnings call up to analysts' questions, CFO Kevan Parekh came forward with the first real piece of bad news.

Parekh said that for the December quarter, which goes through the holiday season, Apple's gross margin will be between 45% and 46%, trailing the 46.3% average analyst estimate, according to LSEG.

"This includes primarily the impact of higher memory costs, as well as some costs related to the ramp of Siri AI," Parekh said.

Apple shares slid about 3% in extended trading, following a drop of almost 2% during regular market hours on Monday.

It's part of an ongoing trend for Apple, which is absorbing some of the soaring costs from the global memory shortage. A margin drop in the fiscal first quarter would be a third straight decline for Apple, following a gross margin of 50.1% in the June period and 47.9% in the September quarter, boosted by 1 percentage point of tariff refunds.

At the midpoint of the forecasted range, it would be Apple's lowest gross margin since the fiscal fourth quarter of 2023.

"What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented," Ternus said on the call, adding that the company is paying more than it did earlier this year. "Looking beyond the December quarter, we see the pricing for memory purchases continuing to increase."

Ternus, who took the reins from Tim Cook on Sept. 1, is grappling with a problem that his predecessor spent his final months addressing. Cook told The Wall Street Journal in June that the memory situation was a "hundred-year flood." Apple followed by raising prices across the board on Macs, iPads, and accessories.

In September, Apple increased the prices of its iPhones by $100, which Ternus said the company did "reluctantly."

Computer and smartphone makers everywhere are suffering. On Friday, Intel had its worst day on the market since the 1970s due to a weak forecast tied to the softening PC market. It's all because of insatiable artificial intelligence demand.

AI server giants like Nvidia and hyperscalers are buying up all the RAM they can find, and they're willing to pay a premium for it, leaving little supply for companies that make smartphones and tablets.

Despite its size and global clout, Apple said it expects supply constraints on iPhones and Macs in the holiday quarter, primarily driven by memory.

On the call, Ternus had some top lieutenants field component-related questions. Sabih Khan, chief operating officer, became the first Apple COO to appear on an earnings call since Cook in 2010, when Steve Jobs was CEO.

"As we have said before, we're seeing less flexibility in the supply chain than normal," Khan told analysts on the call.

In response, Apple has drastically increased its purchase obligations to secure supply, Khan said. Those obligations rose by $33 billion from the June quarter to the September period, Khan said, though he declined to answer questions about whether Apple had signed "long-term agreements" with memory makers.

"We're always working hard to improve supply," Khan said.

Even with the recent margin slide, Apple's gross margins are still well above where they sat earlier in the Cook era, when the number would predictably come in between 38% and 39%. As Apple's profitable services business grew, its gross margin expanded, hitting 40% in 2021 and growing from there.

Analysts see a potential silver lining for Apple in the current market turmoil. Because Apple is more capable of eating a portion of the increased costs, some experts predict the company will take market share from makers of Windows PC and Android phones that don't have such a cushion.

Citing data from a third-party researcher, Ternus said gains are already happening.

"According to IDC, we gained share globally for the quarter and for the full fiscal year," Ternus said. "And we have never sold more Macs in a fiscal year than we did this time, which is a truly remarkable testament to these incredible products."

#88. Pfizer hikes full-year outlook as newer products grow, taps company veteran as new CFO

Pfizer on Tuesday reported third-quarter results that topped estimates and hiked its full-year outlook, as demand for its newly launched and acquired products grows.

Pfizer also Tuesday announced that Cecile Guegan, who has held various finance leadership roles at the company over the last two decades, stepped in as chief financial officer this month. She had served as interim CFO since August, when Pfizer's former CFO, Dave Denton, stepped down to take the same position at Nike.

The company is now expecting full-year revenue to total $62 billion to $64 billion, which compares to a previous outlook of $60.5 billion to $62.5 billion. The low end of that sales range would be roughly flat or down slightly compared with 2025 revenue of $62.6 billion. Still, Pfizer cited its "strong year-to-date performance" as a driver for the guidance hike.

The pharmaceutical giant now expects full-year adjusted profit of between $2.95 and $3.05 per share. That compares with a previous guidance of between $2.80 and $3 per share.

That includes a one-time $650 million charge, or a negative impact of 10 cents per share, recorded in the third quarter tied to the company's licensing and collaboration agreement with Chinese cancer drugmaker Innovent Biologics.

Pfizer is working on a comeback story after its massive pandemic-era success waned. As demand for Covid products drops and generic rivals threaten its biggest moneymakers, CEO Albert Bourla is looking to jumpstart growth through strategic acquisitions, fresh cancer drugs and a big bet on weight loss treatments through its acquisition of Metsera.

Pfizer in August expanded its multi-year cost-cutting efforts to help offset sharp declines in Covid product sales, and is now targeting roughly $9.7 billion in net savings through 2029.

Here's what the company reported for the third quarter compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Tune in at 1:30 p.m. ET as Pfizer CEO Albert Bourla joins CNBC TV to discuss the company's earnings. Watch in real time on CNBC+ or the CNBC Pro stream.

Pfizer reported third-quarter revenue of $16.58 billion, relatively flat from the same period a year ago. The company cited an operational decrease of $57 million, primarily due to lower Covid revenues, and a $15 million negative impact from foreign exchange.

That was offset by strong sales of key products like its blood thinner Eliquis and targeted cancer drug Padcev, which jumped and beat expectations.

Eliquis in particular raked in $2.52 billion in sales, up 25%. Analysts were expecting revenue of $2.40 billion, according to StreetAccount. Padcev posted $720 million in revenue for the period, which compares to the $658 million that analysts were expecting.

Sales of Pfizer's newly launched and acquired products grew 32% operationally. That includes Padcev, its respiratory syncytial virus vaccine Abrysvo, its pneumococcal shot Prevnar 20 and oral lung cancer drug Lorbrena, among several other products.

Pfizer's Covid products also topped estimates for the quarter despite seeing steep sales declines. The company's Covid vaccine generated $808 million in sales, which compares to the $770.4 million that analysts were expecting, StreetAccount estimates show. Meanwhile, Pfizer's antiviral pill, Paxlovid, booked $449 million in sales, coming in above the $394.1 million that analysts were expecting.

The company booked a net income of $3.24 billion, or 56 cents per share, for the period. That compares with net income of $3.54  billion, or 62 cents per share, during the third quarter of 2025.

Excluding certain items, including restructuring charges and costs associated with intangible assets, Pfizer posted earnings per share of 87 cents for the quarter.

#83. Midterm elections could jolt stocks if Democrats sweep Congress

The stock market is not yet pricing in a Democratic sweep of Congress — despite some polls and most prediction markets pointing that way — so that scenario could weigh on share prices Wednesday, according to investors and analysts.

Republicans managing to keep control could cause a short-term relief rally, they said.

Short-term moves aside, the typical post-midterm pattern in the months ahead is bullish. But some wonder if the market could buck the positive historical trend this time because the S&P 500 is already up so much this year going into the election.

As far as certain stocks to watch, a big Democratic win on Tuesday night — especially in the Texas governor race — could add fuel to an AI stock sell-off on the notion that more data center restrictions would gain momentum nationally. Defense stocks could come under pressure on fears of limits on spending.

Polls are pointing to a Democratic victory in the House and a toss-up in the Senate. Prediction markets have been more favorable to Democrats, calling for a blue sweep.

While investors are signaling "begrudging acceptance" to a base case scenario that there will indeed be a Democratic sweep, that's not how they're positioning, said Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research. The firm's recent survey data among more than 100 institutional investors suggests they are not strongly positioned for any particular result.

"Investors seem to have a significant dose of skepticism about whether the final outcome is going to be quite as favorable to Democrats as the polling suggests," Marcus told CNBC, noting that the firm shares the view that the "polling is likely overstating Democratic strength."

JPMorgan sees a blue wave knocking the S&P 500 by about a half percent on Wednesday.

On the other hand, if Republicans end up retaining both chambers of Congress, that could spur a rally, said Sameer Samana, head of global equities and real assets at Wells Fargo Investment Institute.

"From a certainty standpoint, the market knows what to do with Republicans holding both chambers," he said.

Citigroup strategists said Monday that Republicans losing the House but managing to keep the Senate could be "a tailwind for equities into year-end." JPMorgan traders predict up to a 1% pop on Wednesday if Republicans hold on to that chamber.

Looking past any short-term move, stocks tend to rally after the midterms have concluded. In fact, the S&P 500 was up one year out after all midterm elections going back to the early 1980s.

But the S&P 500 has already recorded double-digit gains so far in 2026, so some on Wall Street worry that investors are being overly complacent heading into this year's elections.

During midterm election years, the S&P 500 has typically averaged a 14% drawdown at some point in the second half of the year found Jonathan Krinsky, chief market technician at BTIG.

"The reason stocks often perform well in November during midterms is because [the S&P 500] typically is weak INTO the elections. We have the opposite setup this year," Krinsky wrote last week. "Therefore, while consensus is that we see a post midterm rally, we think the opposite is actually more likely."

It's not helping that the broader market has recently come under significant pressure due to turbulence in the AI trade, which led the Nasdaq Composite to post its worst day since June on Friday. AI shares led the market lower again on Monday.

A strong showing from Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections could pressure the trade even more.

At the state level, increased Democratic control is probably a headwind for stocks associated with data centers and technological capital equipment, analysts said.

"It [would solidify] the early kind of cautionary signs in different states around the advancement of AI infrastructure," Rob Rains at Washington Analysis told CNBC.

Goldman Sachs strategists said this week that a Democratic Congress "might limit data center tax incentives, but a national moratorium looks unlikely in any election outcome."

One example would be Texas — a key hub of the data-center buildout. A surprise loss there by Republican Gov. Greg Abbott has the "potential to spook" the market in the short term, Wolfe's Marcus said.

A blue wave might pressure defense shares, while boosting health care stocks, analysts and investors said.

"A substantial Democratic sweep could limit real spending growth unless a broader agreement increases both defense and health-care funding," said Alex Sagal, global equity analyst at Wells Fargo Investment Institute. However, he noted that the earnings outlook for the defense industry looks "stable to modestly positive."

Wolfe Research's Marcus said that healthcare is going to be "the biggest focus," as Democrats will push for restoring expired Affordable Care Act enhanced premium subsidies or unwinding some of the Medicaid reforms in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

"Healthcare stocks have been doing really well lately," Jed Ellerbroek, portfolio manager at Argent Capital Management, said to CNBC. "I think part of that is the anti-AI trade, but I also think part of it is just optimism that Democrats are taking over."

Ellerbroek is keeping his eye on hospital-related stocks such as HCA Healthcare and Tenet Healthcare as well as health insurers like UnitedHealth and Centene.

Financials was the worst S&P 500 sector in early 2026 on the heels of President Donald Trump calling on Congress to pass a law that would cap credit card interest rates at 10%. While the sector has been able to notch gains since, with its year-to-date rise now at around 3%, things could get more unstable for the group under Democratic control.

"Democrats will come in wanting to show tangible progress on the affordability issue, and that means targeting sectors that are front and center in consumer budgets," said Evercore ISI's Matthew Aks. "We know Trump has populist instincts, populist leanings, and could he pick a couple of issues and decide to team up with the Democrats on those issues and target those industries in a populist way? Absolutely."

Given that Democratic control is often associated with increased regulation, while Republican control is thought to promote deregulation, Democrats could be interested in passing credit card rate caps if they come out victorious.

"I think if Democrats have control of one or both chambers, they would try to pass it and see if they could get Trump to either sign it or force him to veto it, and that would let Democrats kind of score a political point," Aks said.

Trump could restart the war against Iran after holding off any major strikes leading up to the midterms, a move that could upend the overall market and cause energy stocks to outperform. Crude oil prices jumped more than 10% in October, though they gave a lot of that gain back on Monday.

Gasoline prices stood at $4.17 a gallon on average nationwide Sunday, a nearly 40% increase compared to the same period last year, per AAA.

"If there are escalations, that could obviously put upward pressure on prices just ahead of Thanksgiving," said Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysts at Gas Buddy, "which is also when Americans have really staunch traditions in terms of driving to friends and family."

If Trump decides to take forceful action, it could snuff out hope for a typical post-midterm year-end rally.

Evercore ISI said the S&P 500 could retreat by double-digits to end the year at 6,800 as oil spikes if the president "moves to kinetic escalation in Iran ahead of seating the new Congress in January" and Democrats take Congress.

— With reporting by Sarah Min, Tobias Burns and Spencer Kimball
